Composite local observables of sine–Gordon via stochastic analysis

by Massimiliano Gubinelli, Peter Paulovics

University of Oxford

Email: {massimiliano.gubinelli, peter.paulovics}@maths.ox.ac.uk

Abstract

We develop a rigorous framework for the description of general local composite observables in Euclidean quantum field theories. The approach is nonperturbative and combines renormalisation group ideas with techniques of stochastic analysis. Via renormalisation dictated by sufficiently precise approximate solution theory of corresponding flow equations, we construct various local observables of the sine–Gordon model on the full space up to \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\), including highly singular ones such as vertex fields \(\exp (i \alpha \phi (y))\) in the optimal Wick regime \(| \Re e (\alpha) | < 4 \pi / \beta\) and derivatives of arbitrarily high order, i.e. \(\partial^{\mu} \phi (y)\) for any multiindex \(\mu \in \mathbb{N}^2_0\). Applications of the approach include a direct proof of smoothness of observable-inserted correlation functions, and verification that the constructed objects satisfy a version of the prefactorization algebra axioms adapted to our setting.

1Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to develop an understanding of observables of Euclidean (quantum) field theories, i.e. to provide a nonperturbative, probabilistic framework for the construction and analysis of nonlinear functionals of certain generalized random fields. Euclidean field theories are notoriously difficult to construct and describe, in part owing to the need for renormalisation: heuristically, their laws are given by the Radon–Nikodym derivative

\(\displaystyle \text{``} \mathrm{d} \nu (\phi) \propto e \left( - \lambda \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \tilde{V} (\phi (x)) \mathrm{d} x \right) \mathrm{d} \mu (\phi) \text{''}\)

with \(\mu\) a measure on \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\) that is not supported on any space of bona fide functions, thus making the expression \(\tilde{V} (\phi (x))\) for general nonlinear \(\tilde{V}\) a priori ill-defined. To give rigorous meaning to such measures \(\nu\), one therefore needs to understand how the nonlinearities give rise to singularities, and remove them in a way that does not lead to trivial objects.

After a random field \(\varphi_{\infty}\) with \(\operatorname{Law} (\varphi_{\infty}) = \nu\) has been constructed, one would like to study expressions of the form \(\int \prod_j q^{(j)} (\phi (y_j)) \mathrm{d} \nu (\phi)\) with \(y_j \in \mathbb{R}^d\) and \(q^{(j)}\) nonlinear functions. One then finds themselves facing the above renormalisation problem again. What is even worse, the \(q^{(j)}\) are allowed to be much more singular than \(\tilde{V}\), in fact so much that for any field \(\varphi_{\infty}\), it is not guaranteed that after renormalisation, the “correct” object \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} q^{(j)} (\varphi_{\infty} (y_j)) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) exists as an \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)-valued random variable (take, for example, monomials of sufficiently high order derivatives of \(\varphi_{\infty}\)).

Our approach is based on the scale-by-scale coupling of the free field with the interacting EQFT [5]. In this framework, the target random field \(\varphi_{\infty}\) is realised as the terminal value of a field-valued Markov process \((\varphi_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\). The interpretation of the parameter \(t\) is energy scale or resolution; the law of \(\varphi_t\) is the Wilsonian effective description of the interacting field theory. The infinitesimal generator \(\mathscr{L}^V\) of the process is determined by the effective potential \((V_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) of the theory, which in turn is given by the solution of the renormalisation group flow equation.

To renormalise a nonlinear functional \(q\) of the field \(\varphi_{\infty}\), one then needs to study the kernel of the unbounded operator \(\mathscr{L}^V\). Since precise control of the Polchinski equation is in general difficult without a priori knowledge about the theory, we construct scale-dependent functionals \((q_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) such that \(\mathscr{L}^V q \thickapprox 0\) approximately in some appropriate topology. To obtain the full nonperturbative correlation functions, we then employ a probabilistic correction. Indeed, by Ito's formula, the process \(\mathfrak{Q} := (\mathfrak{Q}_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\), where

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{Q}_t := q_t (\varphi_t) + \mathbb{E} \left[ \left. \int_t^{\infty} \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \mathrm{d} s \right| \varphi_t \right], \) (1.1)

is a martingale in the filtration of \(\varphi\). Whenever the martingale \(\mathfrak{Q}\) is closed with \(\sigma (\varphi_{\infty})\)-measurable terminal value, the random variable \(\mathfrak{Q}_{\infty}\) is the functional \(q_{\infty} (\varphi_{\infty})\) of the UV-complete field one is interested in. However, the above martingale, and hence the expectation \(\mathfrak{\langle Q \rangle} := \mathbb{E} [\mathfrak{Q}_0]\), is well-defined even if \(q_{\infty} (\varphi_{\infty})\) fails to exist as a random element of \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\). This latter failure of existence is indeed the case for sufficiently high Euclidean dimension \(d\) or sufficiently singular functional \(q_{\infty}\) (e.g. Wick square of the free field in \(d = 4\), cf. [2], see also [1]). We define the martingales \(\mathfrak{Q}\) as the observables of the theory \(\varphi_{\infty}\), and denote by \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} q (\varphi) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} := \mathfrak{Q}\) where \(q\) is the “classical core” of the functional they arise as renormalisations of; see Section 2 for precise definitions.

We carry out the above program for various observables of the infinite volume massive sine–Gordon model, which is defined as follows. Given a \(T < \infty\), \(\rho \in \mathcal{C}^{\infty}_c (\mathbb{R}^2)\) with \(\rho (x) \in [0, 1]\), \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}\), \(\beta > 0\), denote by

\(\displaystyle \nu_T^{\rho} (\phi) := Z_T^{- 1} \exp \left( - \lambda \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \rho (x) : \cos (\beta \phi (x)) :_T \mathrm{d} x - c_T \right) \mathrm{d} \mu_T (\phi) \) (1.2)

where \(\mu_T\) is the centered Gaussian measure with covariance \(\int_0^T s^{- 2} \exp (- s^{- 1} (- \Delta_{\mathbb{R}^2} + m^2)) \mathrm{d} s\), \(m > 0\), \(: \cdot :_T\) denotes Wick ordering with respect to \(\mu_T\), \(c_T\) is a diverging constant corresponding to vacuum energy renormalisation, and \(Z_T\) is a normalisation constant to keep \(\nu_T\) a probability measure. A limit of \(\{ \nu_T^{\rho} (\phi) \}_{T, \rho}\) as \(T \rightarrow \infty\) and \(\rho \rightarrow \mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{R}^2}\) is called an infinite-volume sine–Gordon(\(\beta^2, m, \lambda\)) measure. The construction of the interacting scale interpolation process \((\varphi_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\), which we take as starting point, was carried out for this model in [29] in the regime \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\). Our first main result is the following

Theorem 1.1. Let \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\), \(m > 0\), \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}\), fix \(y \in \mathbb{R}^2\). There exists a process \((\varphi_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) such that \(\)\(\nu_T^{\rho} (\phi) \rightarrow \operatorname{Law} (\varphi_{\infty})\) weakly in \(H^{- \varepsilon} (\langle x \rangle^{- n})\) for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(n > 2\) as \(T \rightarrow \infty\) and \(\rho \rightarrow \mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{R}^2}\), possibly along a subsequence. The following local observables associated to \(\varphi\) exist:

  • \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \varphi^k (y) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) for any \(k \in \mathbb{N}_0\)

  • \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \partial^{\mu} \varphi (y) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) for any multiindex \(\mu \in \mathbb{N}_0^2\)

  • \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \partial^{\mu} e^{i \alpha \varphi (y)} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) for any \(\alpha \in \mathbb{C}\) with real part \(| \Re e (\alpha) | < 4 \pi / \beta\) and multiindex \(\mu \in \mathbb{N}_0^2\).

In each of these cases, there exist scale-dependent functionals \(q_t\) with \(\lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| q_t (\varphi_t) - \mathfrak{Q}_t \|_{L^1} = 0\).

We emphasize that our method does not require smallness of the coupling constant \(\lambda\) for any purposes.

The condition \(| \Re e (\alpha) | < 4 \pi / \beta\) on the Wick renormalised vertex field \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} e^{i \alpha \varphi (y)} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) is conjectured to be optimal [35]. The way we achieve this condition is through a novel screening mechanism developed for external vertex insertion, i.e. sharp \(L^{\infty}\) bounds on the propagator of the corresponding linearised Polchinski equation; see Section 5.2 and in particular Lemma 5.4 below. This can be considered the main model-specific contribution of the paper.

In our framework, the product of observables \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}\), \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\) is again a martingale, denoted by \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\), that satisfies the approximation property

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| (\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)})_t - \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}_t \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}_t \|_{L^1} = 0.\)

As a consequence, whenever the martingales are closed and therefore their terminal value exists as a random variable, we have

\(\displaystyle \langle \mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)} \rangle = \mathbb{E} [\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_{\infty} \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_{\infty}],\)

a property that any sensible notion of product should satisfy. The details of the definition of the product \(\star\) are given in Section 2.

Denoting the linear span of above observables considered in Theorem 1.1 by \(\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{loc}}_W\), the following result shows that we can construct any finite products of elements of \(\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{loc}}_W\), provided they are local at distinct points. Moreover, we prove that \(\mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{loc}}_W\) admits a structure similar in spirit to the prefactorization algebras of Costello–Gwilliam [18],[19].

Theorem 1.2. Suppose \((\beta^2, m, \lambda)\) satisfy the conditions of Theorem 1.1. For any \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}, \ldots, \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)} \in \mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{loc}}_W\) localized at pairwise distinct points \(y_1, \ldots, y_n \in \mathbb{R}^2\), their product \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \ldots \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)}\) exists. Moreover, the assignment

\(\displaystyle (\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}, \ldots, \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)}) \mapsto \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \ldots \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)}\)

is associative, commutative, \(n\)-linear, and admits identity.

In particular, given disjoint finite index sets \(J, L, M \subseteq \mathbb{N}\), \(\{ k_j \}_{j \in J} \subseteq \mathbb{N}_0\), multiindices \(\{ \mu_l \}_{l \in L} \subseteq \mathbb{N}_0^2\), \(\{ \alpha_m \}_{m \in M} \subseteq \mathbb{C}\) with \(| \Re e (\alpha_m) | < 4 \pi / \beta\), we prove that the correlation functions

\(\displaystyle \left\langle \prod_{j \in J} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \varphi^{k_j} (y_j) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \prod_{l \in L} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \partial^{\mu_l} \varphi (y_l) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \boldsymbol{} \prod_{m \in M} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} e^{i \alpha_m \varphi (y_m)} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \right\rangle \) (1.3)

exist for any \(\{ y_i \}_{i \in J \cup L \cup M} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2\) such that \(y_{i_1} \neq y_{i_2}\) whenever \(i_1 \neq i_2\). It is natural to ask about the smoothness of such correlation functions in the insertion points \(y_i\). As the integral kernel of \((- \Delta + m^2)^{- 1}\) is smooth outside the diagonal, we expect the same property to be inherited for the observable-inserted correlation functions. Given an \(n \in \mathbb{N}\), the big diagonal \(\operatorname{Diag}_n\) is defined as

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Diag}_n := \{ (x_1, \ldots, x_n) \in (\mathbb{R}^2)^n : \exists i \neq j \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \} s.t. \| x_i - x_j \| = 0 \} .\)

We then have the following

Theorem 1.3. Suppose \((\beta^2, m, \lambda)\) satisfy the conditions of Theorem 1.1. For any \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}, \ldots, \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)} \in \mathcal{O}^{\operatorname{loc}}_W\), the correlation functions

\(\displaystyle (y_1, \ldots, y_n) \mapsto \langle \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} (y_1) \star \ldots \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)} (y_n) \rangle\)

are \(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\) on \((\mathbb{R}^2)^n \setminus \operatorname{Diag}_n\).

We note that our proof strategy is general and relies only on the translation invariance of the Gaussian free field.

1.1Related literature

Composite observables are important not only from the viewpoint of physical applications, but also from that of pure mathematics: studying the structure of observables in quantum field theory has inspired progress in functional analysis [30],[3] and various subfields of algebra and geometry [13],[33],[43]. We highlight, in particular, the work of Costello–Gwilliam [18],[19], which in turn served as an inspiration for us to set up the framework of Section 2.4.

As we have mentioned, our approach builds on the scale decomposition process \(\varphi\) of the interacting field theory. This point of view can be traced back to [7]; we refer to [46], [24] for this perspective in the context of fermionic theories. As we have mentioned in the introduction, in the present work we rely on the construction of the sine–Gordon scale decomposition process obtained in [29]. See also the preprint [5] for a conjectural framework for the description of Euclidean field theories based around the process \(\varphi\).

The sine–Gordon model is an important example of a scalar bosonic interacting quantum field theory. The parameter \(\beta\) in (1.2) governs the singularity of the perturbation, and hence the difficulty of the renormalisation problem; in our convention, naive Wick renormalisation of the potential is sufficient when \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\), and the theory is superrenormalisable/subcritical in the regime \(\beta^2 < 8 \pi\). It has a long history of mathematically rigorous study with various methods in various parameter ranges, see e.g. [27], [23], [42], [4], [6], [14], [36] and references therein.

We would like to highlight the continuous-scale renormalisation group approach to the model that relies on convergence of perturbation theory of the effective potential, initiated by the breakthrough work of Brydges–Kennedy [15] in the parameter range \(\beta^2 < 16 \pi / 3\) and extended to \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\) recently by Bauerschmidt–Bodineau [7]. Such perturbation theory is in fact nontrivial owing to the nonpolynomial nature of the cosine,, see the series of papers [12], [38], [37], the recent work [36], and for similar problems in the context of regularity structures, [32] and [16]. Many of the technical difficulties we face in this paper stem from reconciling how this “Mayer-type” perturbation theory behaves in the presence of the external field insertions of Theorem 1.1.

Rigorous construction of composite observables of the sine–Gordon model has been of great interest, in part because of the Coleman correspondence [17], a conjectured duality between massless sine–Gordon and massive Thirring model observables. For partial verification, see [11], [22], [10] and references therein. In the Wick regime \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\) and in the presence of an adiabatic/finite volume cutoff, [26] and [34] studied the insertions of products of first-order derivatives, \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} e^{i \alpha \phi (y)} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) for \(| \alpha | = \beta < \sqrt{4 \pi}\), and the stress-energy tensor. For the massless model in infinite volume at the free fermion point \(\beta^2 = 4 \pi\), the vertex fields \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} e^{i \alpha \phi (y)} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) for \(| \alpha | < \sqrt{4 \pi}\) have been constructed recently in [9] with imaginary multiplicative chaos techniques, and the corresponding one-point function has been shown to satisfy the Lukyanov–Zamolodchikov formula [35]. See Remark 6.3 for a discussion of parameter regimes of \((\beta, \alpha)\) in relation to our construction.

For general scalar bosonic EQFTs, the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms ([40], [41]) imply real analyticity of the Schwinger functions at non-coinciding points (see [28], Chapter 19). As for smoothness of observable-inserted correlation functions, the only proof we are aware of is for the Seiberg bound-constrained vertex operators of Liouville conformal field theory [39], relying on the Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos construction of the model [21]. Our proof is based on a direct flow equation argument, applies for all composite observable insertions we consider, and relies only on translation invariance of the free field. Moreover, the argument itself does not rely on smallness assumption of the coupling \(\lambda\), and hence, in principle can be employed in cases where full verification of the OS axioms is not yet known.

1.2Notation

We write \(\mathbb{N} = \{ 1, 2, \ldots \}\), \(\mathbb{N}_0 = \mathbb{N} \cup \{ 0 \}\), and \(\mathbb{R}_+ = [0, \infty)\). Given an \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0\), denote by

\(\displaystyle 1 : \ell := \{ 1, \ldots, \ell \}, \hspace{4em} 1 : 0 := \emptyset .\)

For index sets \(I_1, I_2 \subseteq 1 : \ell\), we denote their union by \(I_1 \uplus I_2\) instead of \(I_1 \cup I_2\) when \(I_1 \cap I_2 = \emptyset\).

Throughout the paper, unless stated otherwise, \(\rho\) denotes an element of \(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_b (\mathbb{R}^d)\) with \(\rho (x) \in [0, 1]\). We either take \(\rho \equiv 1\) or \(\rho (x) := \rho_R (x) := \varrho (x / L)\) for some \(L > 0\) and \(\varrho\) fixed compactly supported test function.

Given an \(x \in \mathbb{R}^d\) and \(t \geq 0\), set

\(\displaystyle \langle x \rangle := \sqrt{1 + | x |^2}, \hspace{4em} \mathfrak{l}_t := 1 + \log (1 \vee t) .\)

We do not keep track of logarithmic factors throughout the paper, hence the exponent \(c\) of \(\mathfrak{l}_t^c\) may change between occurrences.

Write \(A \lesssim B\) if \(A \leq C B\) for some constant \(C > 0\) independent of the variables and cutoffs under consideration, and \(A \asymp B\) if \(A \lesssim B\) and \(B \lesssim A\). Dependence of \(C\) on parameters is sometimes indicated by subscripts.

For a multiindex \(\mu \in \mathbb{N}^d_0\), denote

\(\displaystyle | \mu | := \sum_{k = 1}^d \mu_k, \hspace{4em} \partial^{\mu} := \prod_{k = 1}^d \partial^{\mu_k}_{z_k}\)

For a Banach space \(E\), we write \(L^p (\Omega ; E)\) for the corresponding Bochner-Lebesgue space.

Given a real Hilbert space \(E\) and a bounded linear functional \(f \in L (E ; \mathbb{R})\), we denote by \(f^{\sharp} \in E\) the unique vector induced by the Riesz–Frechet isomorphism: \(\langle f^{\sharp}, e \rangle_E = f (e)\) for all \(e \in E\). For a Frechet differentiable functional \(f : E \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\), we denote its derivative at \(\phi \in E\) by \(\mathrm{D} f |_{\phi} \in L (E ; \mathbb{R})\), and abbreviate \(( \mathrm{D} f |_{\phi})^{\sharp}\) to \(\mathrm{D} f^{\sharp} |_{\phi}\) or \(\mathrm{D} f |_{\phi}^{\sharp}\).

Once an arbitrary filtration \((\mathscr{F}_t)_{t \geq 0}\) is fixed, we write \(\mathbb{E}_t := \mathbb{E}_t^{\mathscr{F}} := \mathbb{E} [\cdot | \mathscr{F}_t ]\). Conditional expectation with respect to the Brownian filtration \((\mathscr{F}^B_t)_{t \geq 0}\) is denoted by \(\mathbb{E}_t^B\).

2General theory of stochastic observables

2.1Motivation

The basis of our approach to local composite operators is that from a renormalisation group point of view, the natural notion of an observable of a field theory is a martingale with respect to the filtration generated by the multiscale description of the theory, instead of a functional of the interacting field. In this subsection we sketch the motivation behind this idea; the discussion here is semi-informal and rigorous mathematics starts in Section 2.2.

Let \((\dot{G}_u)_{u \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) be such that \(\int_0^{\infty} \dot{G}_u \mathrm{d} u = (- \Delta + m^2)^{- 1}\), and denote by \(\mu_{s, t}\) the centered Gaussian measure with covariance \(G_{s, t} := \int_s^t \dot{G}_u \mathrm{d} u\), \(s \leq t \in [0, \infty]\). Suppose we have access to the effective potentials \((V_t)_{t \in [0, \infty]}\), a family of functionals satisfying the RG equation

\(\displaystyle V_s (\phi) = - \log \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \xi)} \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, t} (\xi) \) (2.1)

for all \(s \leq t \in [0, \infty]\) (see e.g. [8] for a review for mathematicians). We then may define the renormalised/effective measure \(\nu_t\) as

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \nu_t (\phi) := \mathcal{Z}_t^{- 1} e^{- V_t (\phi)} \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, t} (\phi), \) (2.2)

interpreted to be the part of the model that is visible to a detector of resolution at most \(t\). Here the normalisation constant \(\mathcal{Z}_t\) is chosen so that \(\nu_t\) is a probability measure.

One way to think about the RG equation (2.1) is that it gives rise to the scale description process \(\varphi_t\) we mentioned in the introduction, and encodes the Markov property. To see this, define for bounded Borel functions \(h : \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) the linear operator

\(\displaystyle \mathbf{P}_{s, t} h (\phi) := e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \xi)} h (\phi + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, t} (\xi) .\)

Note that we can express taking expectation with respect to the effective measures in terms of the action of \(\mathbf{P}_{s, t}\). Indeed, using (2.1) and (2.2),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} h (\xi) \mathrm{d} \nu_t (\xi) & = & \frac{1}{\int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\xi)} \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, t} (\xi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\xi)} h (\xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, t} (\xi)\\ & = & \frac{1}{e^{- V_0 (0)}} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (0 + \xi)} h (0 + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, t} (0 + \xi)\\ & = & \mathbf{P}_{0, t} h (0) . \end{array}\)

We also see that \(\mathbf{P}_{s, t} h \geq 0\) whenever \(h \geq 0\), \(\mathbf{P}_{s, t} \mathbb{1} = \mathbb{1}\) and, as \(h\) is bounded, \(V_{\infty}\) is assumed to be bounded from below and \(\mu_{s, t}\) is a probability measure, by dominated convergence \(\mathbf{P}_{s, t}\) preserves pointwise convergence. Moreover, as \(\lim_{s \rightarrow t} \mu_{s, t} = \delta_{\xi = 0}\) weakly we obtain \(\mathbf{P}_{t, t} =\operatorname{id}\). Finally, the Chapman–Kolmogorov identity is a consequence of the convolution semigroup property of Gaussian measures: for all \(s \leq u \leq t\) we have \(\mu_{s, t} = \mu_{s, u} \ast \mu_{u, t}\), thus

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbf{P}_{s, u} \mathbf{P}_{u, t} h (\phi) & = & e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_u (\phi + \zeta)} \mathbf{P}_{u, t} h (\phi + \zeta) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, u} (\zeta)\\ & = & e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_u (\phi + \zeta)} e^{V_u (\phi + \zeta)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \zeta + \xi)} h (\phi + \zeta + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{u, t} (\xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, u} (\zeta)\\ & = & e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \eta)} h (\phi + \eta) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, t} (\eta)\\ & = & \mathbf{P}_{s, t} h (\phi) . \end{array}\)

Thus \(\mathbf{P}_{s, t} (\phi, A) := \mathbf{P}_{s, t} \mathbb{1}_A (\phi)\), \(\phi \in \mathcal{S}'\), \(A \in \mathscr{B} (\mathcal{S}')\) defines a Markovian transition kernel describing the evolution of the measures \(\nu_t\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbf{P}_{s, t}^{\ast} \nu_s (A) & := & \int_{\mathcal{S}'} \mathbf{P}_{s, t} \mathbb{1}_A (\phi) \mathrm{d} \nu_s (\phi)\\ & = & \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \xi)} \mathbb{1}_A (\phi + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, t} (\xi) \mathrm{d} \nu_s (\phi)\\ & = & \frac{1}{e^{- V_0 (0)}} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_s (\phi)} e^{V_s (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\phi + \xi)} \mathbb{1}_A (\phi + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{s, t} (\xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, s} (\phi)\\ & = & \frac{1}{e^{- V_0 (0)}} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_t (\psi)} \mathbb{1}_A (\psi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{0, t} (\psi)\\ & = & \nu_t (A) . \end{array} \) (2.3)

Hence, by Kolmogorov's consistency theorem

2.1. All the EQFTs we are interested in are expected to be supported on some weighted Sobolev space of sufficiently low regularity, hence we may replace \(\mathcal{S}'\) with these spaces so that the assumption of Polishness on the state space is satisfied.

2.1 there exists a probability space \((\Omega, \mathscr{A}, \mathbb{P})\) with associated Markov process \((\varphi_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) in its canonical filtration \(\mathscr{F}_t := \sigma (\varphi_s : s \leq t)\) such that \(\operatorname{Law} (\varphi_t) = \nu_t\).

In the usual probabilistic approach to EQFTs ([28], [44]), observables are defined as real-valued functionals \(f_{\infty}\) of \(\varphi_{\infty}\), or equivalently by a Doob–Dynkin argument as \(\sigma (\varphi_{\infty})\)-measurable random variables \(F_{\infty} : \Omega \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\). Given such an observable \(F_{\infty}\), we may define \(F_t\) as conditional expectation with respect to \(\mathscr{F}_t\). Explicitly, setting \(F_t := f_t (\varphi_t)\) with

\(\displaystyle f_t (\phi) := \mathbf{P}_{t, \infty} f_{\infty} (\phi) = e^{V_t (\phi)} \int_{\mathcal{S}'} e^{- V_{\infty} (\phi + \xi)} f_{\infty} (\phi + \xi) \mathrm{d} \mu_{t, \infty} (\xi),\)

by (2.3) we see that for all \(s \leq t\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [f_t (\varphi_t) | \mathscr{F}_s ] = \mathbb{E} [\mathbf{P}_{t, \infty} f_{\infty} (\varphi_t) | \varphi_s ] =\mathbf{P}_{s, t} \mathbf{P}_{t, \infty} f_{\infty} (\varphi_s) =\mathbf{P}_{s, \infty} f_{\infty} (\varphi_s) = f_s (\varphi_s) .\)

In particular, expectation is preserved along the flow:

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mathcal{S}'} f_t (\phi) \mathrm{d} \nu_t (\phi) = \int_{\mathcal{S}'} \mathbf{P}_{t, \infty} f_{\infty} (\phi) \mathrm{d} \nu_t (\phi) = \int_{\mathcal{S}'} f_{\infty} (\phi) \mathrm{d} \mathbf{P}_{t, \infty}^{\ast} \nu_t (\phi) = \int_{\mathcal{S}'} f_{\infty} (\phi) \mathrm{d} \nu_{\infty} (\phi)\)

That is, we constructed a martingale \(F = (F_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) with \(L^1 (\Omega)\)-limit \(F_{\infty}\) such that \(F_t\) is the effective description of the original observable \(F_{\infty}\): it contains details only up to resolution \(t\) and averaging it with respect to the effective measure \(\nu_t\) yields the same result as averaging the original observable \(F_{\infty}\) with respect to the UV-complete EQFT \(\nu_{\infty}\). Thus observables, in the usual sense, may be identified through their multiscale effective description with a subset of martingales with respect to the scale decomposition filtration \(\mathscr{F}\).

It is a well-established fact that the random variable language is insufficient for a proper description of composite observables: it has been shown in [2] that the Wick square of the Gaussian free field, defined through analytic continuation of the Wick square of the relativistic free field, cannot exist as an \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)-valued random variable for \(d \geq 4\). That is, \(\langle : \varphi^2 :, g \rangle_{\mathcal{S}', \mathcal{S}}\), \(g \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\) is not a real-valued random variable, i.e. the problem is not only “spatial” but also “probabilistic” regularity. One expects a similar result for high enough Wick powers also in \(d = 3\). The approach taken in [2] is to regard such objects as \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\)-valued bona fide Hida distributions; see [1] for further discussion on this.

To circumvent the above issue, we thus define observables to be all \(\mathscr{F}\)-martingales. This is a proper generalization to the random variable language: those martingales that are not closed (equivalently, by Doob's martingale convergence theorem, not uniformly integrable) do not correspond to an observable \(F_{\infty}\) in the traditional picture. Intuitively, this generalization has, in some sense, similar flavour to that of classical versus generalized functions: the notion of observable is “completed” by getting rid of uncomfortable convergence issues in the \(t \rightarrow \infty\) limit; yet the rigidity in the structure of a martingale preserves enough information so that important computations can still be carried out. Moreover, as we will see, the multiscale point of view inherent in the martingale description is actually helpful in proving properties of observables.

2.2Definitions and setting

Denote by \(\mathcal{H}\) a Hilbert space densely and continuously embedded into \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d)\), where the latter is endowed with the strong topology. For practical purposes, it is convenient to take \(\mathcal{H} := H^{k, \ell} (\mathbb{R}^d) = B_{2, 2}^k (\langle x \rangle^{- \ell})\) with \(k, \ell > 0\) a polynomially weighted Sobolev space of positive regularity. The parameter \(k\) will be chosen based on the particular observables we are interested in, e.g. we will usually assume that \(\mathcal{H} \hookrightarrow \mathcal{C}\) so that point evaluations \(\delta_x\) are continuous functionals on \(\mathcal{H}\); this can be achieved with \(k > d / 2\) by Morrey's inequality.

Let us fix a probability space \((\Omega, \mathscr{A}, \mathbb{P})\). Consider a filtration \(\mathscr{F} = (\mathscr{F}_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) on \((\Omega, \mathscr{A}, \mathbb{P})\) and a stochastic process \(\varphi = (\varphi_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) adapted to \(\mathscr{F}\) such that \(\varphi_t : \Omega \rightarrow \mathcal{H}\) for every finite \(t\). We assume that \(\varphi_t\) converges at least \(\mathbb{P}\)-a.s. in the weak* topology of \(\mathcal{S}'\) to a random variable \(\varphi_{\infty} : \Omega \rightarrow \mathcal{S}'\). Denote by \(\mathbb{E}_t [\cdot] := \mathbb{E} [\cdot | \mathscr{F}_t]\).

The interpretation of this setting is as follows. \(\operatorname{Law} (\varphi_{\infty})\) is the probability measure we are interested in (usually an EQFT, but note that the framework is more general), the process \(\varphi\) is the scale interpolation process of \(\varphi_{\infty}\) associated to the coarse-graining procedure encoded in \(\mathscr{F}\); that is, \(\varphi_t\) for a \(t < \infty\) is the effective field theory accessible at resolution smaller than \(t\). Finally, \(\mathcal{H}\) is simply the state space of coarse-grained fields.

In the traditional probabilistic setting, observables are real-valued random variables measurable with respect to \(\sigma (\varphi_{\infty})\) and integrable under \(\operatorname{Law} (\varphi_{\infty})\); equivalently, by the Doob–Dynkin representation, we can think of them as functionals of the random field \(\varphi_{\infty}\). We call such objects probabilistic observables. In contrast, we have the following

Definition 2.1. A real-valued

2.2. Strictly speaking then, vertex fields correspond to a pair of observables.

2.2 \(\mathscr{F}\)-martingale is called a stochastic observable .

The set of stochastic observables is denoted by \(\mathscr{O} = \mathscr{O} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathscr{F}, \mathbb{P})\); it naturally admits a vector space structure under \(t\)-wise addition and multiplication by scalars. The space of probabilistic observables is denoted by \(\mathscr{O}_p\); associating to each probabilistic observable \(X\) its Doob martingale \(\mathfrak{X}\), i.e.

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{X}_t := \mathbb{E} [X | \mathscr{F}_t ],\)

it is clear that \(\mathscr{O}_p \subseteq \mathscr{O}\). When no confusion may arise, we shall call stochastic observables simply observables.

Just as probabilistic observables can be thought of functionals of \(\varphi_{\infty}\), we may ask if an observable \(\mathfrak{Q}\) is such that \(\mathfrak{Q}_t\) is also measurable with respect to \(\varphi_t\), so that by Doob–Dynkin we can think of it as a functional of the coarse-grained field \(\varphi_t\) (consider, e.g., the Doob martingales associated to probabilistic observables when \(\varphi\) is a time-homogeneous diffusion and \(\mathscr{F}\) is its natural filtration). This leads us to try to parametrize the space of observables with families of scale-dependent functionals of \(\mathcal{H}\).

Definition 2.2. A function \(q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is said to be a germ for an observable \(\mathfrak{Q}\) if

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - q_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0. \) (2.4)

Based on this definition, there is a natural notion of equivalence relation on the space of scale-dependent functionals: given \(q, \tilde{q} : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\), we write \(q \sim_{\varphi} \tilde{q}\) if

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| \tilde{q}_t (\varphi_t) - q_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0.\)

Denoting by \([q]_{\varphi}\) the corresponding equivalence classes, i.e. \([q]_{\varphi} = q + [0]_{\varphi}\), where

\(\displaystyle [0]_{\varphi} := \{ r : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R} : \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| r_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0 \},\)

the elements of the quotient space are represented by observables: if \(q\) is a germ of an observable \(\mathfrak{Q}\), then all \(\tilde{q} \in [q]_{\varphi}\) are also germs for \(\mathfrak{Q}\), since

\(\displaystyle \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - \tilde{q}_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \leq \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - q (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} + \| \tilde{q} (\varphi_t) - q (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \rightarrow 0 ; \) (2.5)

conversely, if \(q\) is a germ for both \(\mathfrak{Q}, \mathfrak{P} \in \mathscr{O}\), then for all \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - \mathfrak{P}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} & = & \| \mathbb{E}_t [\mathfrak{Q}_T - \mathfrak{P}_T] \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ & \leq & \| \mathfrak{Q}_T - \mathfrak{P}_T \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ & \leq & \| \mathfrak{Q}_T - q_T (\varphi_T) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} + \| \mathfrak{P}_T - q_T (\varphi_T) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \end{array} \) (2.6)

vanishing as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), i.e. \(\mathfrak{Q} = \mathfrak{P}\).

A collection of observables \(\mathcal{Q} := \{ \mathfrak{Q}_t^{(x)} \}_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d}\) is called an observable field. Note that while in our definitions we always took \(\mathbb{R}^d\), it is not hard to generalize the discussion to the case where spacetime is a Riemannian manifold \(M\).

As the notations \(\mathscr{O} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathscr{F}, \mathbb{P})\) and \(\sim_{\varphi}, [q]_{\varphi}\) suggest, the space of observables and the equivalence classes of germs depend on the filtration \(\mathscr{F}\) and the scale decomposition process \(\varphi\). This might seem strange at first, but it is in fact natural: the physically relevant information of an observable is encoded in the \(t = 0\) scale.

Definition 2.3. A state is the linear functional \(\langle \cdot \rangle : \mathscr{O} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) given by \(\langle \mathfrak{O} \rangle := \mathbb{E} [\mathfrak{O}_0]\).

One might think of the situation as follows: we are given a fixed set of abstract symbols \(\mathcal{O} \operatorname{bs}\), containing, for example \(\{ \phi, \phi^n, (\partial^{\mu} \phi)^n, \exp (\phi), \ldots \}\); the choice of \((\Omega, \mathscr{A}, \mathbb{P}, \mathscr{F}, \varphi)\) then induces a partial function \(\pi^{\mathscr{F}, \varphi} : \mathcal{O} \operatorname{bs} \rightarrow \mathscr{O}\) with a corresponding germ structure. What we are really interested in is the set \(\pi^{\mathscr{F}, \varphi} (\mathcal{O} \operatorname{bs})\) of physically relevant observables. Given a different choice \((\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}, \tilde{\varphi})\), as long as both \(\pi^{\mathscr{F}, \varphi}\) and \(\pi^{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}, \tilde{\varphi}}\) are injective and their domain is the same, there exists a bijection between the physically relevant observables under the different representation, i.e. \(\pi^{\mathscr{F}, \varphi} (\mathcal{O} \operatorname{bs}) \backsimeq \pi^{\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}, \tilde{\varphi}} (\mathcal{O} \operatorname{bs})\).

From now on, we will work in the following representation. Consider the heat kernel decomposition of the covariance operator \((- \Delta + m^2)^{- 1}\) of the massive Gaussian free field given by

\(\displaystyle Q_t := \sqrt{\frac{1}{t^2} e^{- \left( \frac{- \Delta + m^2}{t} \right)}},\)

defined via functional calculus or Fourier multipliers. Moreover, denote by \(\dot{G}_t := Q^2_t\). By a standard computation, the \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\) integral kernel \(k^Q_t\) of \(Q_t\) and \(\dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x - y) = k^{Q^2}_t\) of \(\dot{G}_t = Q^2_t\) is given by the formulae

\(\displaystyle k^Q_t (x, y) = \frac{t^{d / 2 - 1}}{(2 \pi)^{d / 2}} e^{- \frac{t}{2} | x - y |^2} e^{- \frac{m^2}{2 t}}, \qquad \operatorname{and} \qquad \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x - y) = k^{Q^2}_t (x, y) = \frac{t^{d / 2 - 2}}{(4 \pi)^{d / 2}} e^{- \frac{t}{4} | x - y |^2} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} \) (2.7)

We can regard \(Q_t \in L (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathcal{H})\) (see e.g. Lemma A.7), denote by \(Q^{\ast}_t : \mathcal{H} \rightarrow L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\) its adjoint, and define the scale interpolation of the free field, an \(\mathcal{H}\)-valued Gaussian process

\(\displaystyle B_t := \int_0^t Q_s \mathrm{d} W_s\)

where \(W\) is a cylindrical Brownian motion on \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\). The fact that the stochastic integral is well-defined and converges to the Gaussian free field \(B_{\infty}\) is well-known, but to be self-contained we prove it in Appendix A along with other basic heat kernel estimates.

For reasons that will be clear soon, we require the following property from the filtration \(\mathscr{F}\).

Definition 2.4. A filtration \(\mathscr{F}\) is called admissible if \(\varphi\) is \(\mathscr{F}\)-adapted and \(B\) is an \(\mathscr{F}\)-martingale.

In the latter part of this paper, we take the scale decomposition process \(\varphi\) to be that of the sine–Gordon model; \(\varphi\) and the admissible filtration \(\mathscr{F}\) is constructed in Theorem 4.4 below.

In general, the basic structure one requires from \(\varphi\) is that it can be represented as a scale-dependent perturbation of the free field process \(B\), i.e. it is an Ito diffusion of the form \(\varphi_t = Z_t + B_t\) where the drift process \(Z\) is

\(\displaystyle Z_t = - \int_0^t \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} V_s (\varphi_s) \mathrm{d} s \) (2.8)

where \(V : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a sufficiently nice scale-dependent functional on the field configuration space \(\mathcal{H}\). The parabolic generator of \(\varphi\), denoted by \(\mathscr{L}^V\), is the operator

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L}^V f_t (\phi) := \partial_t f_t (\phi) + \langle \mathrm{D} f_t |_{\phi}, - \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V_t |_{\phi} \rangle + \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} [Q_t Q^{\ast}_t \mathrm{D}^2 f_t |_{\phi}] \) (2.9)

defined for sufficiently nice \(f : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\). We will frequently abuse notation to write

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{Tr} [Q_t Q^{\ast}_t \mathrm{D}^2 f_t |_{\phi}] =\operatorname{Tr} [\dot{G}_t \mathrm{D}^2 f_t |_{\phi}] = \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} f_t (\phi),\)

see Lemma B.1 and Notation B.2 for justification.

2.3Coherence

Germs serve to parametrize the space of observables. In some cases, the correspondence between observables and germs is bijective: the germ itself completely determines the martingale. The canonical example is when \(q\) solves the backward Kolmogorov PDE: indeed, in this case \(\mathfrak{Q}_t := q_t (\varphi_t)\) is a local martingale. It is convenient to ask for germs that solve the backward equation only approximately in some suitable sense so that the corresponding martingale can still be obtained from the knowledge of \(q\) alone. Such germs are called coherent and this section is concerned with explaining how to “reconstruct” the unique observable from a coherent germ and establish precise conditions that guarantee this can be done. The reconstruction is done via a simple application of Ito's formula, we thus start with recalling some notations from infinite-dimensional stochastic calculus; see e.g. [20] for more details.

Recall that given processes \(Q, f\) satisfying sufficiently strong measurability conditions with \(Q_t \in \mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathcal{H})\) and \(f_t \in \mathcal{H}\) such that \(\mathbb{P} \left( \int_0^T \| Q^{\ast}_t f_t \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)}^2 \mathrm{d} t < \infty \right) = 1\), and setting, for an \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\)-cylindrical Brownian motion \(W\),

\(\displaystyle \int_0^T \langle f_t, Q_t \mathrm{d} W_t \rangle := \int_0^T \tilde{Q}_t^f \mathrm{d} W_t \qquad \operatorname{with} \tilde{Q}_t^f : L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) \rightarrow \mathbb{R} \quad \tilde{Q}_t^f (u) := \langle f_t, Q_t u \rangle_{\mathcal{H}},\)

then \(\| \tilde{Q}_t^f \|_{\mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathbb{R})}^2 = \| Q^{\ast}_t f_t \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)}^2\) and hence the stochastic integral \(\int_0^T \langle f_t, Q_t \mathrm{d} W_t \rangle\) is a well-defined continuous R-valued stochastic process.

Also recall that given an \(\mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathcal{H})\)-valued predictable integrand process Φ, in order for a stochastic integral \(\int_0^T \Phi_t \mathrm{d} W_t\) to be a \(\mathcal{H}\)-martingale, it suffices that \(\Phi_t \in \mathbb{H}^2_W ([0, T] ; \mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathcal{H}))\), i.e. that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \int_0^T \| \Phi_t \|_{\mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathcal{H})}^2 \mathrm{d} t < \infty .\)

Thus for the stochastic integral \(\int_0^T \langle f_t, Q_t \mathrm{d} W_t \rangle\) to be a real-valued martingale, it suffices that

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} \int_0^T \| \tilde{Q}_t^f \|_{\mathcal{L}_2 (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) ; \mathbb{R})}^2 \mathrm{d} t = \mathbb{E} \int_0^T \| Q^{\ast}_t f_t \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)}^2 \mathrm{d} t < \infty .\)

Given a \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\), we denote by \(q^T\) the restriction of \(q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) to \([0, T] \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\).

Definition 2.5. A functional \(q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is called coherent if

  1. For each \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\), the functional \(q^T\) is continuous on \([0, T] \times \mathcal{H}\), and the partial derivatives \(\partial_t\), \(\mathrm{D}\), \(\mathrm{D}^2\) are bounded and continuous on bounded subsets of \([0, T] \times \mathcal{H}\).

  2. For each \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\), we have \(\mathbb{E} \int_0^T \| Q^{\ast}_t \mathrm{D} q^T_t |_{\varphi_t}^{\sharp} \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)}^2 \mathrm{d} t < \infty\), where \(Q\) is the operator in the heat kernel interpolation.

  3. It holds that

    \(\displaystyle \int_0^{\infty} \| \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \mathrm{d} s < \infty . \) (2.10)

Lemma 2.6. (reconstruction) Suppose \(q\) is a coherent functional and \(\varphi\) is an Ito diffusion with generator \(\mathscr{L}^V\). Then

  1. For each \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\) there exists a unique observable \(\mathfrak{Q}^{\{ T \}}\) defined as \(\mathfrak{Q}_t^{\{ T \}} := q_t^T (\varphi_t) + r^q_{t, T}\) for \(t \in [0, T]\) and extended to \(\mathbb{R}_+\) by\(\) \(\mathfrak{Q}_T^{\{ T \}}\), where

    \(\displaystyle r^q_{t, T} := \mathbb{E}_t \int_t^T \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \mathrm{d} s. \) (2.11)

  2. There exists a unique observable \(\mathfrak{Q}\) with germ \(q\) defined as \(\mathfrak{Q}_t := q_t (\varphi_t) + r^q_{t, \infty}\) such that

    \(\displaystyle \lim_{T \rightarrow \infty} \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - \mathfrak{Q}_t^{\{ T \}} \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0\)

    for each \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\).

Proof. As we have already indicated, this is a simple application of Ito's formula.

  1. Indeed, fixing \([s, t] \subseteq [0, T]\), by condition 1. we can apply Ito's formula to obtain

    \(\displaystyle q_t^T (\varphi_t) = q_s^T (\varphi_s) + \int_s^t \mathscr{L}^V q_u^T (\varphi_u) \mathrm{d} u + \int_s^t \langle \mathrm{D} q_u^T |_{\varphi_u}^{\sharp}, Q_u \mathrm{d} W_u \rangle .\)

    By condition 2. and admissibility, the last term is a martingale, thus we have that

    \(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_s \mathfrak{Q}_t^{\{ T \}} = \mathbb{E}_s \left[ q_s^T (\varphi_s) + \int_s^t \mathscr{L}^V q_u^T (\varphi_u) \mathrm{d} u + \int_s^t \langle \mathrm{D} q_u^T |_{\varphi_u}^{\sharp}, Q_u \mathrm{d} W_u \rangle \right] + \mathbb{E}_s \mathbb{E}_t \int_t^T \mathscr{L}^V q_u^T (\varphi_u) \mathrm{d} u = q_s^T (\varphi_s) + \mathbb{E}_s \int_s^t \mathscr{L}^V q_u^T (\varphi_u) \mathrm{d} u + \mathbb{E}_s \mathbb{E}_t \int_t^T \mathscr{L}^V q_u^T (\varphi_u) \mathrm{d} u = \mathfrak{Q}^{\{ T \}}_s\)

    so \(\mathfrak{Q}^{\{ T \}}\) is a martingale. The condition (2.4) is satisfied trivially.

  2. For each \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), for all \(T \geq t\)

    \(\displaystyle \mathfrak{Q}_t - \mathfrak{Q}_t^{\{ T \}} = \mathbb{E}_t \int_T^{\infty} \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \mathrm{d} s\)

    so that (2.10) yields by conditional Jensen inequality, Fubini and dominated convergence that

    \(\displaystyle \| \mathfrak{Q}_t - \mathfrak{Q}_t^{\{ T \}} \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \leq \left\| \int_T^{\infty} \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \mathrm{d} s \right\|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \leq \int_{\mathbb{R}_+} \mathbb{1}_{[T, \infty)} \| \mathscr{L}^V q_s (\varphi_s) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \mathrm{d} s \rightarrow 0\)

    as \(T \rightarrow \infty\). Since the \(\mathfrak{Q}^{\{ T \}}\) are martingales, this implies that \(\mathfrak{Q}\) is also a martingale. Similarly, (2.4) follows from (2.10).

\(\Box\)

Let us fix some terminology for objects we see in the construction for future reference.

Definition 2.7. The observable \(\mathfrak{Q}\) of the previous lemma is called the reconstruction of \(q\).

It is clear from the linearity of \(\mathscr{L}^V\) and the triangle inequality that coherent functionals form a vector space. Moreover, the corresponding linear structure induced on a subset of observables via the Ito reconstruction “lift” is compatible with the linear structure of \(\mathscr{O}\) (i.e. \(t\)-wise addition and scalar multiplication). More precisely, we make the following

Definition 2.8. Denote by \(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}\) the vector space of coherent functionals. Define the reconstruction map \(\mathfrak{R}\) as

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{R} : \widehat{\mathscr{O}} \rightarrow \mathscr{O}, \qquad (\mathfrak{R} (q))_t := q_t (\varphi_t) + r^q_{t, \infty} .\)

The reconstruction map is linear: given \(q^{(a)}, q^{(b)} \in \widehat{\mathscr{O}}\), linearity of the generator \(\mathscr{L}^V\) yields

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} (\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} + q^{(b)}))_t & = & (q^{(a)} + q^{(b)})_t (\varphi_t) + r^{q^{(a)} + q^{(b)}}_{t, \infty}\\ & = & q^{(a)}_t (\varphi_t) + r^{q^{(a)}}_{t, \infty} + q^{(b)}_t (\varphi_t) + r^{q^{(b)}}_{t, \infty}\\ & = & (\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)}))_t + (\mathfrak{R} (q^{(b)}))_t\\ & = & (\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)}) + \mathfrak{R} (q^{(b)}))_t \end{array}\)

so that \(\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} + q^{(b)}) = \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)}) + \mathfrak{R} (q^{(b)})\); homogeneity is also trivial.

Definition 2.9. Let \(\mathscr{O}_c := \operatorname{Ran} (\mathfrak{R}) \subseteq \mathscr{O}\) be the linear subspace of \(\mathscr{O}\) consisting of those observables that are Ito reconstructions of coherent germs.

The above reconstruction reduces the existence of composite observables to the construction of coherent functionals \(q\), which in turn depends largely on estimates of the scale decomposition process \(\varphi\) and its generator \(\mathscr{L}^V\). Once one has a sufficient control over these objects, the construction of coherent germs can be achieved in a systematic manner based on ideas of renormalisation group flow equations, see Section 2.6 below.

This shift in perspective of putting coherent functionals in the center of attention validates the following slight abuse of terminology: from now on, we will often call a scale-dependent functional \(q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) a germ (without specifying the corresponding martingale \(\mathfrak{Q}\) for which (2.4) holds); the tacit assumption is that such a functional will turn out to be coherent, and thus indeed a germ (in the strict sense of Definition 2.2) of its reconstructed observable.

Remark 2.10. (Connection to RG flow equations) Heuristically, it is easy to see how our framework connects to Wilsonian ideas. Suppose we are searching for the family of pairs \((V_t, \tilde{q}_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) such that the perturbed interaction

\(\displaystyle V^{\theta}_t := V_t + \theta \tilde{q}_t \) (2.12)

satisfies the RG equation (2.1). One obtains from the resulting flow of perturbed renormalised densities \((e^{- V^{\theta}_t})_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) the evolution of the effective description of the observable: indeed,

\(\displaystyle - \partial_{\theta} e^{- V^{\theta}_t} |_{\theta = 0} = \tilde{q}_t e^{- V_t},\)

which yields the expectation of \(q_t\) under the effective measure, cf. (2.2). By a standard argument, we get from (2.1) the observable-perturbed Polchinski equation

\(\displaystyle \partial_t V^{\theta}_t + \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} V^{\theta}_t = \frac{1}{2} \langle \mathrm{D} V^{\theta}_t, \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V^{\theta}_t \rangle .\)

Taking \(\theta = 0\) we obtain the usual Polchinski equation for the potential \(V_t\), while taking \(\partial_{\theta}\) at \(\theta = 0\) gives the observable flow equation

\(\displaystyle \partial_t \tilde{q}_t + \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \tilde{q}_t = \langle \mathrm{D} \tilde{q}_t, \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V_t \rangle,\)

which is precisely the backward Kolmogorov PDE \(\mathscr{L}^V \tilde{q} = 0\).

In light of (2.12), the functional \(\tilde{q}_t\) has the geometric interpretation of a tangent vector at \(V_t\) to the (formal) manifold of effective interactions \(\mathcal{M}\). The Polchinski equation gives rise to a vector field \(\beta\) on \(\mathcal{M}\):

\(\displaystyle \beta (V_t) := - \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} V_t + \frac{1}{2} \langle \mathrm{D} V_t, \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V_t \rangle .\)

The family \((V_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) is then an integral curve of this vector field, and the evolution of \(\tilde{q}_t \in T_{V_t} \mathcal{M}\) describes how a tangent vector is transported along such a curve by \(\beta\). Our martingales \(\mathfrak{Q}\) contain the information of such a flow in an abstract way, without having to give meaning to \(\mathcal{M}\): indeed, for each \(t\), we can employ a Doob–Dynkin representation to obtain a functional \(\mathfrak{Q}_t = \tilde{q}_t (\varphi_t)\). Allowing for coherent functionals \((q_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) in this picture has the interpretation of a family of approximate tangent vectors that are sufficiently close to the actual \(\tilde{q}\) such that the defect can be recovered via the correction procedure of Lemma 2.6.

2.4Observable product

Observables of a quantum field theory do not form an algebra: we do not expect to be able to multiply any two observables. From a mathematical point of view, in the Euclidean setting this is ultimately a consequence of the singularity of the covariance of the random field \(\varphi_{\infty}\) on the diagonal in \(d \geq 2\). From a physics perspective, it is related to the fact that the effect of a measurement (i.e. coupling of a measurement device with the physical system) on a quantum system is considerable, hence taking two measurements of a quantum particle simultaneously yields incoherent results. Thus in most axiomatic frameworks of quantum field theory (see e.g. Haag–Kastler–Araki axioms [30],[3] factorization algebras [18],[19]), there is a notion of locality: “combining” measurements should be possible only for observables that are supported on disjoint spacetime regions.

It is well-known that the space of martingales is not stable under taking pointwise (with respect to \(t\)) products. To have a well-defined notion of observable product in the stochastic observable language, we leverage the fact that germs parametrize the space of observables: given two observables, we can multiply their respective germs. The idea is then to use the reconstruction map \(\mathfrak{R}\) to push forward the product to the space of observables; we note the analogy with theory of regularity structures [31].

When the product germ happens to be coherent, by the Ito reconstruction lemma it gives rise to a unique observable, which we take to be our definition of the product observable provided it approximates the pointwise products of observables in \(L^1 (\Omega)\) in the UV. In our setting, the failure of the algebra structure is then encoded in the nontrivial condition that the product germ is coherent.

Definition 2.11. Let \(\{ \mathfrak{Q}^{(j)} \}_{j = 1}^n\) be observables with coherent germs \(\{ q^{(j)} \}_{j = 1}^n\), respectively, and write \(q^I := \prod_{i \in I} q^{(i)}\). If for every nonempty \(I \subseteq 1 : n\), we have that

  1. \(q^I\) is coherent,

  2. for any partition \(P\) of \(I\),

    \(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \left\| \mathfrak{R} (q^I)_t - \prod_{J \in P} \mathfrak{R} (q^J)_t \right\|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0, \) (2.13)

then we say that the tuple \((q^{(1)}, \ldots, q^{(n)})\) is compatible and call the martingale \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \ldots \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(n)} := \mathfrak{R} (q^{1 : n})\) the observable product.

Remark 2.12. The UV \(L^1 (\Omega)\) approximation property (2.13) is what intuition expects the product should behave like: we would like to have, for all \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), that

\(\displaystyle (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_t = \lim_{T \rightarrow \infty} \mathbb{E}_t [\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_T \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_T] \) (2.14)

in \(L^1 (\Omega)\), and when the martingales \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}, \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}, \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\) are closed, to have

\(\displaystyle \langle \mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)} \rangle = \mathbb{E} [\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_{\infty} \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_{\infty}] . \) (2.15)

The statements (2.14) and (2.15) follow from (2.13) trivially: indeed,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \mathbb{E}_t [\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_T \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_T] - (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} & = & \| \mathbb{E}_t [\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_T \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_T - (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_T] \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ & \leq & \| \mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_T \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_T - (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_T \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \end{array}\)

vanishing as \(T \rightarrow \infty\); closedness of \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}\) implies

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_{\infty} - \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}_t \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} & \leq & \| (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_{\infty} - (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ & + & \| (\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_t - \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}_t \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \end{array}\)

vanishing as \(t \rightarrow \infty\), which, coupled with \(\mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_t \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_t \rightarrow \mathfrak{O}^{(1)}_{\infty} \mathfrak{O}^{(2)}_{\infty}\) in probability (implied by convergence of \(\mathfrak{O}^{(j)}\)), yields that \((\mathfrak{O}^{(1)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(2)})_{\infty} = \mathfrak{Q}^{(1)}_{\infty} \mathfrak{Q}^{(2)}_{\infty}\) almost surely.

The formulae of the above remark are not the only reason for requiring the condition (2.13). Let \(q^{(a)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(a)}\) coherent germs for \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(a)}\) and \(q^{(b)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(b)}\) are coherent germs for \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(b)}\) such that \(q := q^{(a)} q^{(b)}\) and \(p := p^{(a)} p^{(b)}\) are coherent. To have a well-defined notion of the observable product, we then want that \(p\) and \(q\) reconstruct the same martingale, i.e. that \(\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)}) = \mathfrak{R} (p^{(a)} p^{(b)})\). Leaving condition (2.13) out of the definition of compatibility leaves us unable to prove this consistency result in general: there is no guarantee that \(q^{(a)} q^{(b)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(a)} p^{(b)}\).

Proposition 2.13. If \(q^{(a)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(a)}\) are coherent germs for \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(a)}\) and \(q^{(b)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(b)}\) are coherent germs for \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(b)}\) such that \((q^{(a)}, q^{(b)})\) and \((p^{(a)}, p^{(b)})\) are compatible, then \(q^{(a)} q^{(b)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(a)} p^{(b)}\). Consequently, \(\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)}) = \mathfrak{R} (p^{(a)} p^{(b)})\).

Proof. Indeed,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \| q^{(a)}_t q^{(b)}_t (\varphi_t) - p^{(a)}_t p^{(b)}_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ \leq & \| q^{(a)}_t q^{(b)}_t (\varphi_t) - \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)})_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} + \| \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)})_t - \mathfrak{Q}^{(a)}_t \mathfrak{Q}^{(b)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ + & \| p^{(a)}_t p^{(b)}_t (\varphi_t) - \mathfrak{R} (p^{(a)} p^{(b)})_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} + \| \mathfrak{R} (p^{(a)} p^{(b)})_t - \mathfrak{Q}^{(a)}_t \mathfrak{Q}^{(b)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \end{array}\)

vanishes as \(t \rightarrow \infty\) by coherence and compatibility. Combining the fact that \(q^{(a)} q^{(b)} \sim_{\varphi} p^{(a)} p^{(b)}\) with (2.5) and (2.6) yields \(\mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)}) = \mathfrak{R} (p^{(a)} p^{(b)})\).\(\Box\)

In light of the discussion above, we do not expect the product \(\star\) to be a well-defined binary operation on the space \(\mathscr{O}_c\) of observables possessing coherent germs. Regardless, it is well-defined as a partial function and is easily seen to satisfy the following properties.

Proposition 2.14. The partial binary operation \(\star : \mathscr{O}_c \times \mathscr{O}_c \rightharpoonup \mathscr{O}_c\) is

  1. commutative in the following sense: let \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)}, \mathfrak{O}^{(b)} \in \mathscr{O}_c\) with coherent germs \(q^{(a)}, q^{(b)}\), respectively. Then \((q^{(a)}, q^{(b)})\) is compatible if and only if \((q^{(b)}, q^{(a)})\) is compatible, and when they are it holds that

    \(\displaystyle \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)} = \mathfrak{O}^{(b)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} .\)

  2. distributive in the following sense: if \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)}, \mathfrak{O}^{(b)}, \mathfrak{O}^{(c)} \in \mathscr{O}_c\) are such that \((q^{(a)}, q^{(b)})\) and \((q^{(a)}, q^{(c)})\) are compatible, then \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)},\) \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}\) and \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)} + \mathfrak{O}^{(c)})\) exist and

    \(\displaystyle \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)} + \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}) = \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)} + \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)} .\)

  3. associative in the following sense: if \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)}, \mathfrak{O}^{(b)}, \mathfrak{O}^{(c)} \in \mathscr{O}_c\), are such that \((q^{(a)}, q^{(b)}, q^{(c)})\) is compatible, then

    \(\displaystyle (\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)}) \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)} = \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)} = \mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}) .\)

  4. unital in the following sense: there exists a unique observable \(\mathfrak{1} = (\mathfrak{1}_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} \in \mathscr{O}_c\) given by \(\mathfrak{1}_t := 1\) such that \(\mathfrak{O} \star \mathfrak{1}\) exists for all \(\mathfrak{O} \in \mathscr{O}_c\) and \(\mathfrak{O} \star \mathfrak{1} = \mathfrak{O} \).

Proof. Commutativity, associativity and unitality are trivial.

For distributivity, note that by linearity of \(\mathscr{L}^V\) and the triangle inequality, we have that \(q^{(a)} (q^{(b)} + q^{(c)})\) is coherent. Hence

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \| \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} (q^{(b)} + q^{(c)}))_t - \mathfrak{O}^{(a)}_t (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)}_t + \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}_t) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ \leq & \| \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(b)})_t - \mathfrak{O}^{(a)}_t \mathfrak{O}^{(b)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} + \| \mathfrak{R} (q^{(a)} q^{(c)})_t - \mathfrak{O}^{(a)}_t \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}_t \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} \end{array}\)

vanishing as \(t \rightarrow \infty\), yielding existence of \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)} + \mathfrak{O}^{(c)})\).\(\Box\)

Remark 2.15. The reverse implication for distributivity is not true, as seen by the following simple example. Let \(\varphi_t := B_t\) be the scale interpolation of the free field and let \(x \in \mathbb{R}^d\), \(d \geq 2\). Consider the germs \(q^{(a)} = q^{(b)} := \delta_x\) and \(q^{(c)} := - \delta_x\). These are coherent and the corresponding observables are \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(a)} = \mathfrak{Q}^{(b)} = B_t (x)\) and \(\mathfrak{Q}^{(c)} = - B_t (x)\). The germ \(q^{(a)} (q^{(b)} + q^{(c)}) = 0\) is coherent, the observable \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star (\mathfrak{O}^{(b)} + \mathfrak{O}^{(c)})\) exists and is the constant zero martingale. However, \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(b)}\) and \(\mathfrak{O}^{(a)} \star \mathfrak{O}^{(c)}\) do not exist, in the sense that e.g. \(q^{(a)} q^{(b)} = \delta_x^2\) is not coherent; renormalisation is needed to define such a product.

2.5Germ subspaces

It is important to know when the product of observables exists. The previous definitions suggest to introduce subspaces of germs that help determining whether they are compatible. The rest of this section is concerned with setting up such machinery.

Given \(M \in \mathbb{N}\), \(\boldsymbol{\alpha} = (\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_M) \in \mathbb{C}^M\), \(\boldsymbol{y} = (y_1, \ldots, y_M) \in (\mathbb{R}^d)^M\), denote by

\(\displaystyle \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \phi (\boldsymbol{y}) \rangle := \sum_{j = 1}^M \alpha_j \phi (y_j) .\)

Moreover, given \(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_a \in \mathbb{C}^{M_a}, \boldsymbol{\alpha}_b \in \mathbb{C}^{M_b}\) we define their join by

\(\displaystyle \boldsymbol{\alpha}_a \veebar \boldsymbol{\alpha}_b := (\alpha_{a, 1}, \ldots, \alpha_{a, M_a}, \alpha_{b, 1}, \ldots, \alpha_{b, M_b}) \in \mathbb{C}^{M_a + M_b}\)

and similarly \(\boldsymbol{y}_a \veebar \boldsymbol{y}_b\). Obviously

\(\displaystyle \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}_a \veebar \boldsymbol{\alpha}_b, \phi (\boldsymbol{y}_a \veebar \boldsymbol{y}_b) \rangle = \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}_a, \phi (\boldsymbol{y}_a) \rangle + \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}_b, \phi (\boldsymbol{y}_b) \rangle . \) (2.16)

Definition 2.16. Let \(\kappa, \sigma \in \mathbb{R}\), \(c \geq 0\), \(p \in [1, \infty]\), \(M \in \mathbb{N}\), \(\boldsymbol{\alpha} \in \mathbb{C}^M\), \(\boldsymbol{y} \in (\mathbb{R}^d)^M\). Consider the seminorm

\(\displaystyle \| q \|_{\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p, \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{y}}^{\kappa, \sigma, c}} := \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} \langle t \rangle^{- \kappa} \mathfrak{l}_t^{- c} \| e^{- \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \varphi_t (\boldsymbol{y}) \rangle} q_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^p (\Omega)} + \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} \langle t \rangle^{\sigma + 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^{- c} \| e^{- \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \varphi_t (\boldsymbol{y}) \rangle} \mathscr{L}^V q_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^p (\Omega)}\)

for a germ \(q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) such that \(\mathscr{L}^V q\) is well-defined. Define the associated subspaces of germs

\(\displaystyle \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p, \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{y}}^{\kappa, \sigma, c} := \{ q : \mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathcal{H} \rightarrow \mathbb{R} : \| q \|_{\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p, \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{y}}^{\kappa, \sigma, c}} < \infty \}\)

We call the parameter \(\kappa \in \mathbb{R}\) the superficial degree of divergence, and \(\sigma > 0\) the accuracy of perturbative approximation.

The \(\boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{y}\) in the notation is frequently suppressed when this does not lead to confusion. Clearly, for any \(\varepsilon > 0\), we have

\(\displaystyle \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p + \varepsilon}^{\kappa, \sigma, c} \subseteq \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c}, \qquad \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c} \subseteq \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa + \varepsilon, \sigma, c}, \qquad \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c} \subseteq \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma - \varepsilon, c}, \qquad \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c} \subseteq \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c + \varepsilon} . \) (2.17)

Denote

\(\displaystyle \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma} := \bigcup_{c \geq 0} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c}, \hspace{4em} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\infty -}^{\kappa, \sigma, c} := \bigcap_{p \in [1, \infty)} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c}, \hspace{4em} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\infty -}^{\kappa, \sigma} := \bigcap_{p \in [1, \infty)} \bigcup_{c \geq 0} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c} .\)

Note that this definition of the \(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\infty -}^{\kappa, \sigma}\) space allows for \(p\)-dependent logarithmic corrections, as opposed to the smaller space \(\bigcup_{c \geq 0} \bigcap_{p \in [1, \infty)} \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_p^{\kappa, \sigma, c}\). The applications we consider in this paper do not require this stronger property, hence for our purposes the weaker space suffices, even though the germs we will construct in fact lie in the stronger one.

The purpose of the objects introduced is the quantitative description of compatibility.

Lemma 2.17. (factorization lemma) Let \(\kappa_j, \sigma_j, p_j, M_j, \boldsymbol{\alpha}_j, \boldsymbol{y}_j\), \(j \in 1 : n\), be as in Definition 2.16, and denote by \(\boldsymbol{\alpha} = \boldsymbol{\alpha}_1 \veebar \ldots \veebar \boldsymbol{\alpha}_n\), \(\boldsymbol{y} = \boldsymbol{y}_1 \veebar \ldots \veebar \boldsymbol{y}_n\). Suppose \(q^{\langle j \rangle} \in \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p_j, \boldsymbol{\alpha}_j, \boldsymbol{y}_j}^{\kappa_j, \sigma_j}\), and for all \(j_1 \neq j_2 \in 1 : n\) we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+} \langle t \rangle^{\sigma + 1} \left\| e^{- \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \varphi_t (\boldsymbol{y}) \rangle} \operatorname{Tr} [\dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} q^{\langle j_1 \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \mathrm{D} q^{\langle j_2 \rangle}_t (\varphi_t)] \prod_{j \in 1 : n \setminus \{ j_1, j_2 \}} q^{\langle j \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} < \infty\)

for a \(p \in [1, \infty]\), \(\sigma \in \mathbb{R}\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle \sum_{j \in 1 : n} p_j^{- 1} \leq p^{- 1}, \qquad \sigma \leq \bigwedge_{j \in 1 : n} \left( \sigma_j - \sum_{m \in (1 : n) \setminus \{ j \}} \kappa_m \right) .\)

Then \(\prod_{j = 1}^n q^{\langle j \rangle} \in \widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{p, \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \boldsymbol{y}}^{\kappa, \sigma}\) for any \(\kappa\) with \(\sum_{j \in 1 : n} \kappa_j \leq \kappa\).

Proof. Clearly

\(\displaystyle \langle t \rangle^{- \sum_{j = 1}^n \kappa_j} \left\| \prod_{j \in 1 : n} q^{\langle j \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} \leq \prod_{j \in 1 : n} \langle t \rangle^{- \kappa_j} \| q^{\langle j \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^{p_j} (\Omega)} .\)

The generator of the product can be bounded as

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \langle t \rangle^{\sigma + 1} \left\| \mathscr{L}^V \left( \prod_{j \in 1 : n} q^{\langle j \rangle}_t \right) (\varphi_t) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)}\\ \leq & \sum_{j = 1}^n \langle t \rangle^{\sigma_j + 1} {\| \mathscr{L}^V q^{\langle j \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^{p_j} (\Omega)}} \prod_{m \neq j} \langle t \rangle^{- \kappa_m} \| q^{\langle m \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \|_{L^{p_m} (\Omega)}\\ + & \sum_{j < k} \langle t \rangle^{\sigma + 1} \left\| \operatorname{Tr} [\dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} q^{\langle j \rangle}_t \mathrm{D} q^{\langle k \rangle}_t] \prod_{m \notin \{ j, k \}} q^{\langle m \rangle}_t (\varphi_t) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} . \end{array}\)

Coupled with (2.16) and (2.17), the statement follows.\(\Box\)

Remark 2.18. The reason for the exponential weights \(e^{- \langle \boldsymbol{\alpha}, \varphi_t (\boldsymbol{y}) \rangle}\) in Definition 2.16 is to allow for 2 dimensional theories in the general framework, where observables of the form \(\exp (\alpha \phi (y))\) play an important role. In turn, such exponentials do not behave well with the Hölder inequalities of the above lemma (consider e.g. the \(d = 2\) Gaussian free field) and require slightly different treatment. Since we are going to apply the general theory on such a 2 dimensional model, we will see how the general case of allowing exponentials work and closes the arguments; for theories and observables that are not of this generality, one may always take \(\boldsymbol{\alpha} = 0\) as a special case.

2.6The strategy of the construction

In order to find \(q\) such that (2.10) holds, we need to have a good understanding of the solution theory of the observable flow equation \(\mathscr{L}^V q = 0\), i.e.

\(\displaystyle \partial_s q_s + \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_s} q_s = \langle \mathrm{D} q_s, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} V_s \rangle \) (2.18)

To do this, we construct \(\ell^{\ast}\)-th order approximate germs with a Picard iteration scheme: we let

\(\displaystyle q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} := \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} q^{[\ell]}\)

where \(q^{[0]} \equiv 0\), and \(q^{[\ell]}\) for \(\ell \geq 1\) is defined to be the solution of

\(\displaystyle \partial_s q_s^{[\ell]} + \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} [\dot{G}_s \mathrm{D}^2 q_s^{[\ell]}] = \sum_{\ell' + \ell'' = \ell} \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell']}_s, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} V_s^{[\ell'']} \rangle \) (2.19)

with appropriate boundary conditions (see the next section), where the \(V^{[\ell]}\) are iterates of a Picard scheme similar to (2.19) of the Polchinski equation for the potential \(V\); namely, \(V^{[0]} \equiv 0\) and for \(\ell \geq 1\), \(V^{[\ell]}\) solves

\(\displaystyle \partial_s V_s^{[\ell]} + \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} [\dot{G}_s \mathrm{D}^2 V_s^{[\ell]}] = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\ell' + \ell'' = \ell} \langle \mathrm{D} V^{[\ell']}_s, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} V_s^{[\ell'']} \rangle . \) (2.20)

Set \(V^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} := \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} V^{[\ell]}\) and write \(F^{[\ell]} := \mathrm{D} V^{[\ell]}\).

We are now going to show semi-heuristically that solving (2.19) up to high enough order \(\ell^{\ast}\) gives a good approximating germ, i.e. for which \(\mathscr{L}^V q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} \approx_{\ell^{\ast}} 0\) in a suitable sense.

Suppose there exists an \(\tilde{\ell} \in \mathbb{N}\) such that for any \(\ell' \geq \tilde{\ell}\), the canonical Ito remainder associated to the germ \(F^{[\leq \ell']} (y)\), denoted by \(r^{F^{[\leq \ell']}}\), exists, and

\(\displaystyle \lim_{t \rightarrow \infty} \| F^{[\leq \ell']}_t (\varphi_t) (y) - F^{[\leq \tilde{\ell}]} (\varphi_t) (y) \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} = 0. \) (2.21)

Denote by \(\tilde{\ell}\) the smallest of such integers. Combining (2.21) with (2.5) yields that all truncations with \(\ell' \geq \tilde{\ell}\) reconstruct the same observable (field), which we denote by \(\{ F_s (y) \}_{s \geq 0}\) and call the effective force.

For any \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0\), write

\(\displaystyle \dot{G} R^{[> \ell]} := \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} \dot{G} r^{F^{[\leq \ell]}} & \operatorname{if} & \ell \geq \tilde{\ell}\\ \sum_{\ell'' = \ell + 1}^{\tilde{\ell}} \dot{G} F^{[\ell'']} + \dot{G} r^{F^{[\leq \tilde{\ell}]}} & \operatorname{if} & \ell < \tilde{\ell} . \end{array} \right. \) (2.22)

Lemma 2.19. Letting \(q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} := \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} q^{[\ell]}\) where \(q^{[0]} \equiv 0\), and \(q^{[\ell]}\) for \(\ell \geq 1\) is defined via (2.19), we have

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L}^V q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} = - \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell]}, \dot{G} R^{[> (\ell^{\ast} - \ell)]} \rangle . \) (2.23)

Proof. To simplify notation, write \(F^{[\ell]}_s := F^{[\ell]}_s (\varphi_s)\) and similarly for \(q^{[\ell]} .\) Then Ito reconstruction and (2.22) gives that for arbitrary \(\ell^{\ast} \in \mathbb{N}_0\) we have

\(\displaystyle \dot{G} F = \dot{G} F^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} + \dot{G} R^{[> \ell^{\ast}]} . \) (2.24)

Thus compute with (2.9) and (2.19) that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathscr{L}^V q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} & = & \left( \partial_s + \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}} \right) q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} - \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}, \dot{G} F^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]} \rangle - \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}, \dot{G} R^{[> \ell^{\ast}]} \rangle\\ & = & \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{lll} \ell' + \ell'' & = & \ell\\ \ell, \ell', \ell'' & \in & \mathbb{N}_0 \end{array}$}}} \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell']}, \dot{G} F^{[\ell'']} \rangle - \left\langle \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell]}, \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \dot{G} F^{[\ell]} \right\rangle - \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}, \dot{G} R^{[> \ell^{\ast}]} \rangle\\ & = & - \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{lll} \ell', \ell'' & \leq & \ell^{\ast}\\ \ell' + \ell'' & \geq & \ell^{\ast} + 1 \end{array}$}}} \langle \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell']}, \dot{G} F^{[\ell'']} \rangle - \left\langle \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \mathrm{D} q^{[\ell]}, \dot{G} R^{[> \ell^{\ast}]} \right\rangle . \end{array}\)

Pick an \(\ell' \leq \ell^{\ast}\). Then the first sum runs over all the \(\dot{G} F^{[\ell'']}\) with

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} \ell'' + \ell' & \geq & \ell^{\ast} + 1\\ \ell'' & \leq & \ell^{\ast} \end{array} \right\} \Leftrightarrow \ell^{\ast} - \ell' + 1 \leq \ell'' \leq \ell^{\ast},\)

thus

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \sum_{\ell'' = \ell^{\ast} - \ell' + 1}^{\ell^{\ast}} \dot{G} F^{[\ell'']} + \dot{G} R^{[> \ell^{\ast}]} & = & \sum_{\ell'' = \ell^{\ast} - \ell' + 1}^{\ell^{\ast}} \dot{G} F^{[\ell'']} + (\dot{G} F - \dot{G} F^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]})\\ & = & \dot{G} F - \dot{G} F^{[\leq (\ell^{\ast} - \ell')]}\\ & = & \dot{G} R^{[> (\ell^{\ast} - \ell')]} \end{array}\)

where we used (2.24) twice.\(\Box\)

Within the rest of this subsection, we tune the discussion to the \(d = 2\) sine–Gordon model in regime \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\); nevertheless, the ideas introduced are general at their core and should extend to other models mutatis mutandis.

Suppose that there exists a fixed \(\kappa \geq 0\), the superficial degree of divergence of the observable (cf. Definition 2.16), for which we can prove the formal estimate

\(\displaystyle \| \mathrm{D} q_s^{[\ell]} \|_{L^p (\Omega)} \lesssim \langle s \rangle^{\kappa - (\ell - 1) \delta} \) (2.25)

where \(\delta := 1 - \frac{\beta^2}{8 \pi} \in (0, 1]\) is the distance from criticality, and that we also have the bound

\(\displaystyle \| \dot{G}_s R_s^{[> \ell]} \|_{L^{\infty} (\Omega ; L^{\infty})} \lesssim \langle s \rangle^{- \delta - \ell \delta - 1} \) (2.26)

modulo logarithmic corrections.

Plugging these into (2.23), using Holder and Young's inequalities and \(\| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s \|_{L^1} \lesssim \langle s \rangle^{- 2}\), we formally obtain

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| \mathscr{L}^V q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}_s \|_{L^1 (\Omega)} & \leq & \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \| \langle \mathrm{D} q_s^{[\ell]}, \dot{G}_s R^{[> (\ell^{\ast} - \ell)]}_s \rangle \|_{L^1 (\Omega)}\\ & \lesssim & \sum_{\ell \leq \ell^{\ast}} \langle s \rangle^{\kappa - (\ell - 1) \delta} \langle s \rangle^{- \delta - (\ell^{\ast} - \ell) \delta - 1}\\ & \lesssim & \langle s \rangle^{\kappa - \ell^{\ast} \delta - 1} . \end{array} \) (2.27)

Since \(\delta > 0\), choosing \(\ell^{\ast}\) large enough then yields an arbitrarily precise approximate solution \(q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}_s\) of the equation \(\mathscr{L}^V q = 0\), i.e. we can construct germs \(q^{[\leq \ell^{\ast}]}_s \in \widehat{\mathscr{O}}^{\kappa, \sigma}_{\infty -}\) with arbitrarily high accuracy of perturbative approximation \(\sigma\) (cf. Definition 2.16). The construction of observables and observable products will then follow from the general theory developed so far, in particular the factorization lemma (Lemma 2.17) and Ito reconstruction (Lemma 2.6).

Note that in the above argument we relied on the postulated bounds (2.21) and (2.26) which of course need to be proven first before we can move on to the construction of various germs \(q\). It is not too difficult to see that in fact these can be obtained in a similar vein to what we have explained just now for general observables, by proving (2.25) for the germ of the force \(F\) with superficial degree of divergence \(\kappa := 1 - \delta\).

To construct the effective force and hence the sine-Gordon measure, in the regime \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\), it is sufficient to develop the equation (2.20) up to order \(\ell^{\ast} = 3\), and in general, to some \(\ell^{\ast} (\beta^2)\) that blows up as we approach criticality \(\beta^2 \rightarrow 8 \pi\). However, since our observables can have arbitrarily high superficial degree of divergence \(\kappa\), we are forced to exploit the high order cancellations in Lemma 2.19, and hence need to develop the iterative scheme to arbitrarily high \(\ell^{\ast}\) for any \(\beta^2 > 0\); indeed, cf. (2.27).

The following sections carry out the above roadmap rigorously. Section 3 is concerned with studying (2.20); as a consequence of the bounds derived there, Section 4 constructs the effective force, and verifies (2.21) and (2.26). Section 5 constructs \(q^{[\ell]}\) for various observables by solving the equation (2.19). Finally, Section 6 finishes the construction and proves Theorems 1.1 and 1.2.

2.7Renormalisation via flow equations

Constructing observables of a field theory requires additional renormalisation on top of the renormalisation of the model itself. Indeed, taking general nonlinear functionals \(Q (\varphi_{\infty})\) of an already-renormalised EQFT \(\varphi_{\infty}\) might introduce new divergences that need to be accounted for. An interesting aspect of these additional singularities is that they can be especially severe: in the language of previous sections, the superficial degree of divergence \(\kappa \geq 0\) can be arbitrarily large. In contrast, power counting arguments suggest that the renormalisation of the model itself needs to take care of only relatively mild singularities.

Curing the aforementioned arbitrarily severe singularities, in general, requires introducing arbitrarily many (but finite) renormalisation counterterms; moreover, the form of such counterterms for complicated nonlinearities may be quite non-obvious. Hence in order to get a complete theory of composite observable renormalisation, it is desirable to approach the introduction of counterterms in a systematic and fully automated manner. The requirement of propagating the inductive bounds (2.25) in \(\ell\) through the flow equation (2.19) not only offers such a way, but in fact forces it upon us. The counterterms are generated through an appropriate choice of boundary conditions on the flow, which we now explain.

We will construct the germs by making the ansatz

\(\displaystyle q^{[\ell], T}_t (\phi) = {\sum_{\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}}} \langle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}, f^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\phi) \rangle \) (2.28)

where \(\gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\) are \(\phi\)-independent kernels, \(f^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\phi)\) are functionals of the field, and \(\boldsymbol{a}\) are multiindices carrying information about the expansion, containing e.g. reference to \(\ell\). We require that only finitely many kernels of the sum are nonzero.
