Plugging the above ansatz into (2.19), matching the kernels corresponding to the same basis element \(f^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\phi)\) we obtain a hierarchy of linear ODEs of the form

\(\displaystyle \partial_t \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} + \dot{\mathbb{U}}_t \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} = - \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\)

for some \(t\)-dependent coefficient function \(\dot{\mathbb{U}}_t\) and source term \(\mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\). The latter is a sum over \(n\)-linear functionals of kernels \(\gamma^{(\boldsymbol{d})}_{t, T}\). Using the Duhamel principle, we rewrite the equation into mild form

\(\displaystyle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} = e^{\mathbb{U}_{t, T}} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{T, T} + \int_t^T e^{\mathbb{U}_{t, s}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \mathrm{d} s \) (2.29)

In order to show an estimate of the form (2.25), we set up the ansatz (2.28) in a way so that the hierarchy (2.29) is triangular in \(\boldsymbol{a}\); we then solve the system inductively and try to propagate a bound of the form

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980} \lesssim \langle t \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \) (2.30)

with \(\mathfrak{l}_t := 1 + \log (1 \vee t)\), where \(c\) is a constant that we do not track and may change from line to line, and \(\unicode{x2980} \cdot \unicode{x2980}\) is a suitable norm compatible with the structure of the flow equation. To this end, we will aim to show that the integrand of (2.29) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}e^{\mathbb{U}_{t, s}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \unicode{x2980} \lesssim \langle s \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \) (2.31)

Kernels with \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} < 0\) are called irrelevant: they are expected to remain bounded under the flow and hence get a trivial UV boundary condition. In contrast, kernels with \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \geq 0\) need explicit renormalisation and are called relevant. Thus in general for \(\ell \geq 2\) we aim to impose the boundary conditions

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{T, T} = 0 & \operatorname{if} & \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} < 0\\ \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{0, T} = 0 & \operatorname{if} & \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \geq 0. \end{array} \) (2.32)

It is clear that the case \(\ell = 1\) is slightly different, since it has no contributions from lower iteration orders, as \(q^{[0]} \equiv 0\).

When \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} < 0\), we flow backward: the boundary condition at \(t = T\) yields the equation

\(\displaystyle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} = \int_t^T e^{\mathbb{U}_{t, s}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \mathrm{d} s \) (2.33)

which propagates the inductive bound (2.30) using (2.31):

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980} \lesssim \int_t^{\infty} \langle s \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \langle t \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_t^c .\)

In the \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \geq 0\) case we flow forward: plugging into (2.29) the relevant boundary condition (2.32) at \(t = 0\) we obtain

\(\displaystyle e^{\mathbb{U}_{0, T}} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{T, T} = - \int_0^T e^{\mathbb{U}_{0, s}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \mathrm{d} s,\)

which, since \(- \mathbb{U}_{0, T} + \mathbb{U}_{0, s} = - \mathbb{U}_{s, T}\), means that \(\gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{T, T} = - \int_0^T e^{- \mathbb{U}_{s, T}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \mathrm{d} s\). Plugging this back into (2.29) yields, as \(- \mathbb{U}_{s, T} + \mathbb{U}_{t, T} = {\mathbb{U}_{t, s}} \),

\(\displaystyle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} = - \int_0^t e^{\mathbb{U}_{t, s}} \mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T} \mathrm{d} s. \) (2.34)

which again propagates the inductive bound (2.30), via (2.31):

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980} \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980} \lesssim \int_0^t \langle s \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \langle t \rangle^{\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}} \mathfrak{l}_t^c .\)

In the relevant case \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \boldsymbol{a} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \geq 0\), we expect from (2.34) the kernel \(\gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\) to blow up as \(t \rightarrow \infty\); coupled with the corresponding basis element, \(\langle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}, f^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\phi) \rangle\) is a renormalisation counterterm. Moreover, the uniform-in-\(T\) bounds allow for inductively taking the limit \(T \rightarrow \infty\) in the source terms \(\mathscr{S}^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{s, T}\), rendering the counterterm \(\langle \gamma^{(\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, \infty}, f^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\phi) \rangle\) also independent of the UV cutoff \(T\).

3The Polchinski equation of sine–Gordon

In this section we study the Polchinski equation (2.20). The aim is twofold: first, since the observable flow equation (2.19) contains \(V^{[\ell]}\), we expect that proving the source term bound (2.31) for kernels of the germ \(q\) requires a good understanding of kernels of the potential germ \(V\). Second, as we have already noted, closing the argument in Section 2.6 requires access to the estimates (2.21) and (2.26) which need to be proven first. While the analysis of the Polchinski equation of sine–Gordon in the regime \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\) is not novel, see e.g. [7], [29], we include a fully self-contained treatment here partly to fix notation, and partly because we use it to develop ideas, techniques and motivation that we will use in analysis of the observable flow equations later on.

As a warmup, we first solve the equation (2.20) in the Wick regime \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\) by choosing the ansatz (2.28) to be the usual Brydges–Kennedy Mayer expansion. We then explain how the regime \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\), because of the presence of relevant kernels, requires localisation and flowing forward, and hence induces a generalised kernel representation. Doing so, we prove a sharp screening estimate on the covariance matrix of the Mayer representation that will turn out to be crucial in the construction of vertex field observables.

3.1Warmup: the Wick regime \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\)

Given a field configuration \(\phi \in \mathcal{H}\), we denote by

\(\displaystyle \psi_{\xi} := \psi_{(\sigma, x)} := \exp (i \beta \sigma \phi (x)) \qquad \operatorname{where} \quad \xi := (\sigma, x) \in \{ - 1, 1 \} \times \mathbb{R}^2\)

a vertex field with charge \(\sigma \in \{ - 1, 1 \}\) at position \(x \in \mathbb{R}^2\). Given a finite index set \(I \subseteq \mathbb{N}\), we denote by \(\sigma_I := (\sigma_i)_{i \in I}\), \(x_I := (x_i)_{i \in I}\), \(\xi_I := (\sigma_I, x_I)\) and define

\(\displaystyle \chi (\xi_I) := \sum_{j \in I} \sigma_j\)

the total charge of the ensemble \(\xi_I\). We write \(1 : \ell := \{ 1, \ldots, \ell \}\). The representation (2.28) for the potential \(V^{[\ell], T}\) is, at first, the Mayer expansion ansatz

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} V^{[\ell], T}_t (\phi) & := & \int v^{[\ell]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \psi_{1 : \ell} \rho (x_{1 : \ell}) \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : \ell}\\ & := & \sum_{\sigma_{1 : \ell} \in \{ - 1, 1 \}^{\ell}} \int_{(\mathbb{R}^2)^{\ell}} v^{[\ell]}_{t, T} (x_{1 : \ell}, \sigma_{1 : \ell}) \prod_{j = 1}^{\ell} e^{i \beta \sigma_j \phi (x_j)} \prod_{j = 1}^{\ell} \rho (x_j) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : \ell} . \end{array} \) (3.1)

where \(\rho \in \mathcal{C}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2)\), \(\rho (x) \in [0, 1]\) serves as a volume cutoff. The UV cutoff \(T\) in the notation of the kernels \(v^{[\ell]}_{t, T}\) will be frequently suppressed when it does not lead to confusion. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, all estimates will be uniform in \(\rho\) and \(T\).

Plugging the above ansatz into the Polchinski equation (2.20), by the Duhamel principle we obtain a mild equation for the kernels of the representation. That is, denoting by

\(\displaystyle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) := - \frac{\beta^2}{2} \sum_{j \in I_1} \sum_{k \in I_2} \sigma_j \sigma_k \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_j - x_k), \qquad \operatorname{and} \qquad \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_I) := \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_I, \xi_I), \) (3.2)

for index sets \(I_1, I_2, I \subseteq \mathbb{N}\), we have the following simple

Lemma 3.1. Suppose that the kernels \(v^{[\ell]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell})\) satisfy the equation

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} v^{[\ell]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) & = & e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} v^{[\ell]}_{T, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell})\\ & - & \int_t^T e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \sum_{I_1 \uplus I_2 = 1 : \ell} C_{| I_1 |, | I_2 |} v_{s, T}^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_{s, T}^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \mathrm{d} s \end{array} \) (3.3)

for some positive combinatorial coefficients \(C_{| I_1 |, | I_2 |}\), where the sum runs over all \(I_1, I_2 \subseteq 1 : \ell\) such that \(I_1 \cap I_2 = \emptyset\) and \(I_1 \cup I_2 = 1 : \ell\). Then \(V^{[\ell], T}_t\), given by (3.1), satisfies the Polchinski equation (2.20).

Proof. A straightforward computation; see Appendix B.2.\(\Box\)

Our goal is now to carry out the program of Section 2.7, i.e. to propagate an inductive bound on the kernels through the equation (3.3) by a proper choice of boundary conditions. To do this, we have to define the norm \(\unicode{x2980} \cdot \unicode{x2980}\) in which we estimate.

Given \(x_I := \{ x_i \}_{i \in I} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d\) for a finite index set \(I \subseteq \mathbb{N}\), denote the Steiner distance by

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{St} (x_I) := \min_{x_I \subseteq x_J} \min_{\tau \in c (x_J)} L (\tau)\)

where \(L (\tau)\) is the (Euclidean) length of a tree \(\tau\) on \(\mathbb{R}^d\), and the second minimum runs over the set of spanning trees \(c (x_J)\) of the points \(x_J\), i.e. over all trees that connect all points of \(x_J\). For a nonnegative function \(\Theta\) that might depend on information about the kernel \(v^{[\ell]}\), in particular \(\ell\), and that we will determine later, we then define the weight

\(\displaystyle \omega_u^{\Theta} (x_I) := \exp \left( \Theta \sqrt{u} \operatorname{St} (x_I) \right) \) (3.4)

and the associated Banach norm

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell]}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} := \sup_{\xi_1} \int | v^{[\ell]}_t (\xi_{1 : \ell}) | \omega_t^{\Theta} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{1 : \ell}) \rangle^{\eta} \mathrm{d} \xi_{2 : \ell} . \) (3.5)

for some \(\eta \geq 0\) to be specified later.

The Steiner weight \(\omega_u^{\Theta}\) forces exponential localization of the kernels as we approach the UV; its role will become apparent later on. For the renormalised potential itself, we will need it for technical reasons when \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\) in Section 3.2 below; for constructing the sine–Gordon model alone, we could have taken \(\Theta := 0\) in the Wick regime \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\). However, even in this regime, proving the existence of general observable products require UV localization of their germs (see Lemma 6.5 below), which in turn needed to be inherited from the potential sector.

The scale-independent weight \(\langle \operatorname{St} (x_{1 : \ell}) \rangle^{\eta}\) is required to control the infinite-volume limit for the observables. We will sometimes suppress the \(\eta\) and \(\Theta\)-dependence in the notation when it does not lead to confusion, and also frequently take \(\Theta := \varsigma \in \mathbb{R}_+\) constant.

It is seen inductively from (3.3) that the kernels \(v^{[\ell]}_t\) are invariant under permutations under the index set \(1 : \ell\), hence in fact

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell]}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} = \sup_{\xi_j} \int | v^{[\ell]}_t (\xi_{1 : \ell}) | \omega_t^{\Theta} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{(1 : \ell)}) \rangle^{\eta} \mathrm{d} \xi_{(1 : \ell) \setminus \{ j \}}\)

for any \(j \in 1 : \ell\). The following simple lemma shows that these norms are compatible with the structure of the flow equation (3.3).

Lemma 3.2. Let \(\varsigma, \eta \in \mathbb{R}_+\) be arbitrary. Then

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_t^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \lesssim_{\beta} \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_1 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_2 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \langle t \rangle^{- 2} \) (3.6)

Proof. Indeed, given any trees \(\tau_i\) connecting \(x_{I_i}\) and \(\tau\) an edge between a point in \(x_{I_1}\) and \(x_{I_2}\), we have that \(\tau_1 \cup \tau_2 \cup \tau\) connects \(x_{I_1 \uplus I_2}\), thus

\(\displaystyle \operatorname{St} (x_{I_1 \uplus I_2}) = \min_{x_{I_1 \uplus I_2} \subseteq x_J} \min_{\tau \in c (x_J)} L (\tau) \leq \min_{x_{I_1} \subseteq x_J} \min_{\tau \in c (x_J)} L (\tau) + \min_{x_{I_2} \subseteq x_J} \min_{\tau \in c (x_J)} L (\tau) + \tilde{d} (x_{I_1}, x_{I_2})\)

where \(\tilde{d} (x_{I_1}, x_{I_2}) := \inf_{(a, b) \in x_{I_1} \times x_{I_2}} | a - b |_{\ell^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)}\). It then follows from the triangle inequality that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega_t^{\varsigma} (\xi_{I_1 \uplus I_2}) & \leq & \omega_t^{\varsigma} (\xi_{I_1}) \omega_t^{\varsigma} (\xi_{I_2}) e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} \tilde{d} (x_{I_1}, x_{I_2})}\\ \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{I_1 \uplus I_2}) \rangle^{\eta} & \lesssim_{\eta} & \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{I_1}) \rangle^{\eta} \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{I_2}) \rangle^{\eta} \langle \tilde{d} (x_{I_1}, x_{I_2}) \rangle^{\eta} . \end{array} \) (3.7)

Then Young's convolution inequality gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_t^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta}\\ \leq & \sup_{\xi_1} \int \left| v_t^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \omega_t^{\varsigma} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} \tilde{d} (x_{I_1}, x_{I_2})} v_t^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \omega_t^{\varsigma} (\xi_{I_2}) \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{1 : \ell}) \rangle^{\eta} \right| \mathrm{d} \xi_{2 : \ell}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_1 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \unicode{x2980}v_t^{[| I_2 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \sup_y \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \left| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x - y) e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} | x - y |} \langle x - y \rangle^{\eta} \right| \mathrm{d} x. \end{array}\)

We then conclude from Lemma A.2.\(\Box\)

We impose the boundary conditions

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{llll} v_{0, T}^{[1]} & : = & \frac{\lambda}{2} & \\ v^{[\ell]}_{T, T} & : = & 0 & \operatorname{for} \ell \geq 2. \end{array} \) (3.8)

The motivation for the \(\ell = 1\) boundary condition is that at resolution \(t = 0\), inserting it into (3.1) we obtain

\(\displaystyle V^{[1], T}_0 (\phi) = \sum_{\sigma_1 \in \{ - 1, 1 \}} \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \frac{\lambda}{2} e^{i \beta \sigma_1 \phi (x_1)} \rho (x_1) \mathrm{d} x_1 = \lambda \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \cos (\beta \phi (x)) \rho (x) \mathrm{d} x\)

the expected form of the sine–Gordon potential with coupling \(\lambda\). The boundary conditions at \(\ell \geq 2\) follow from the naive power counting argument of Section 2.7, see the proof of the following

Lemma 3.3. Let \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi \Leftrightarrow \delta > \frac{1}{2}\). Take \(\varsigma \in \mathbb{R}_+\) arbitrary. With the boundary conditions (3.8), the system of equations (3.3) admits a solution satisfying

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell]}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \asymp \langle t \rangle^{1 - \ell \delta} \) (3.9)

where \(\lambda_t := \frac{| \lambda |}{2} e^{\frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_t (0)}\).

Proof. For ℓ=1, since \(v^{[0]}_{\cdot, T} \equiv 0\), the source term of (3.3) vanishes, and plugging in \(t = 0\) we obtain

\(\displaystyle \frac{\lambda}{2} = v_{0, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) = v_{T, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) e^{\mathbb{G}_{0, T} (\xi_1)} = v_{T, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) e^{- \frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{0, T} (0)}\)

which yields \(v_{T, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) = \frac{\lambda}{2} e^{\frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{0, T} (0)}\); plugging it back to (3.3) gives

\(\displaystyle v_{t, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) = v_{T, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, T} (\xi_1)} = \frac{\lambda}{2} e^{\frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{0, T} (0)} e^{- \frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{t, T} (0)} = \frac{\lambda}{2} e^{\frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{0, t} (0)} .\)

It satisfies, for any \(\varsigma \geq 0\)

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v_{t, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} = \lambda_t \sup_{x_1} \left| e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} 0} \right| = \lambda_t \asymp e^{\frac{\beta^2}{8 \pi} \log (1 \vee t)} \asymp \langle t \rangle^{1 - \delta},\)

verifying the bound (3.9) at \(\ell = 1\). Hence from now on we assume \(\ell \geq 2\). With the irrelevant boundary condition \(v^{[\ell]}_{T, T} = 0\), we estimate (3.3) via Lemma 3.2 and the inductive assumption by

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell]}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \sum_{I_1 \uplus I_2 = 1 : \ell} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_s^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \leq & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \sum_{I_1 \uplus I_2 = 1 : \ell} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |]} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_s^{[| I_2 |]} (\xi_{I_2}) \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \sum_{I_1 \uplus I_2 = 1 : \ell} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |]} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda_s {(\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{| I_1 | - 1}} \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \lambda_s {(\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{| I_2 | - 1}} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathrm{d} s. \end{array}\)

where we used that \(t \leq s\) to bound the norms. Now as \(x \mapsto \dot{\mathcal{G}}_u (x)\) is a positive definite function, with \(t \leq s\) it follows that \(\| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lesssim 1\). Moreover, the assumptions \(\delta > 1 / 2\) and \(\ell \geq 2\) combined with

\(\displaystyle \lambda_s^{\ell} \langle s \rangle^{- \ell} \asymp \langle s \rangle^{- \ell \delta}\)

make the above integral convergent, verifying (3.9).\(\Box\)

3.2Extension to \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\)

When \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\), the proof of Lemma 3.3 breaks down. Based on the discussion of Section 2.7, one might naively try to change boundary conditions when the backward integral diverges and flow the corresponding kernels forward. Ultimately this will turn out to be the solution, however the implementation requires us to be careful and first make two remarks about the structure of the flow equation. First, we note that some kernels are in fact irrelevant in the entire subcritical regime:

Example 3.4. (2-point case) Even if \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\), the \(\ell = 1\) case of Lemma 3.3 goes through without modification, so that in the \(\ell = 2\) case (3.3) reads, up to constants and the volume cutoff \(\rho\), as

\(\displaystyle v^{[2]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \asymp v^{[2]}_{T, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2})} - \int_t^T e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \lambda_s^2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} s. \) (3.10)

Note that \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})\) depends on the total charge \(\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) := \sigma_1 + \sigma_2 \in \{ - 2, 0, 2 \}\) of the contribution:

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2}) := - \beta^2 (\mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) + (\mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) \neq 0} - \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0}) \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_1 - x_2)) .\)

For the charged contributions \(| \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) | = 2\), the particles repel each other and the supremum is attained as \(x_1 - x_2 \rightarrow \infty\) (recall that \(t \leq s\) and \(x \mapsto \dot{\mathcal{G}}_u (x)\) is positive):

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x_{1 : 2}} - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_1 - x_2) \leq - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) .\)

On the other hand, in the neutral case \(| \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) | = 0\) the particles attract each other and form a dipole, cancelling the \(- \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0)\) gain:

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x_{1 : 2}} - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) + \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_1 - x_2) \leq 0.\)

Thus

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x_{1 : 2}} e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \lesssim (\lambda_t \lambda_s^{- 1})^{| \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) |} . \) (3.11)

Hence for charged contributions we gain from the covariance matrix \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})\) compared to the naive bound \(\| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lesssim 1\) we had in the previous proof and this gain is sufficient to restore integrability and flow backward. In the neutral case, the naive bound was in fact sharp, the integral in (3.10) is divergent and the kernel is relevant.

The following analysis on the screening mechanism of \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s}\) generalizes the estimate (3.11) to the \(\ell\)-point case. Since it serves as a base point to the more general case of the external vertex field insertion of Section 5.2, we decide to include a full self-contained proof.

Lemma 3.5. (Individual screening) Let \(t \leq s\). Then there exists a constant \(C\) such that for all \(x_g, x_b, z \in \mathbb{R}^2\) with \(| x_g - x_b | \leq | x_b - z |\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_b - z) - \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_g - z) \leq C.\)

Proof. See Appendix A.2.\(\Box\)

We can bootstrap the above estimate by recursively extracting neutral pairs from \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s}\).

Lemma 3.6. (Screening bound on \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s}\)) Let \(t \leq s\). Then

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x_{1 : \ell}} e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \lesssim_{\ell} \lambda_t^{| \chi (\xi_{1 : \ell}) |} \lambda_s^{- | \chi (\xi_{1 : \ell}) |} . \) (3.12)

Proof. For simplicity denote \(\chi := \chi (\xi_{1 : \ell})\). First suppose the ensemble is purely charged, i.e. \(| \chi | = \ell\). Then \(\sigma_j \sigma_k = 1\) for all \(j, k \in 1 : \ell\), so

\(\displaystyle e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} = \exp \left( - \ell \frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) - \beta^2 \sum_{j < k} \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_j - x_k) \right) \leq \exp \left( - \ell \frac{\beta^2}{2} \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) \right) = \lambda_t^{\ell} \lambda_s^{- \ell}, \) (3.13)

verifying (3.12). Suppose from now on that \(| \chi | \leq \ell - 1\); we will show that we can remove neutral clusters from \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})\) until we arrive at a purely charged subset of particles \(\tilde{\xi}_{1 : | \chi |} \in (\{ - 1, 1 \} \times \mathbb{R}^2)^{| \chi |}\) and so that

\(\displaystyle e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \lesssim e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\tilde{\xi}_{1 : | \chi |})} ; \) (3.14)

the statement then follows from applying (3.13) to the purely charged sector \(\tilde{\xi}_{1 : | \chi |}\).

Since \(\)\(| \chi | \leq \ell - 1\), there is at least one neutral pair, i.e. \(j_+, j_- \in 1 : \ell\) such that \(\sigma_{j_+} \sigma_{j_-} = - 1\). Denote by \(k_+, k_-\) the index of a closest of such neutral pairs, i.e. a pair \(\eta := \{ (+ 1, x_{k_+}), (- 1, x_{k_-}) \} \in (\{ - 1, 1 \} \times \mathbb{R}^2)^2\) such that

\(\displaystyle | x_{k_+} - x_{k_-} | = \min_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} j_+, j_- : \sigma_{j_+} \sigma_{j_-} = - 1 \end{array}$}}} | x_{j_+} - x_{j_-} | .\)

By possibly relabeling the points, we distinguish the purely charged core and the ensemble of neutral pairs with \(\eta\) being the last, i.e. we assume \(\sigma_1, \ldots, \sigma_{| \chi |} =\operatorname{sgn} (\chi)\) and \(\sigma_{| \chi | + j} = - \sigma_{| \chi | + j + 1}\) for all \(j \in 1, \ldots, \ell - | \chi | - 1\) and \(k_- \neq k_+ \in \{ \ell - 1, \ell \}\) The covariance matrix factorizes accordingly:

\(\displaystyle e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} = e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : (\ell - 2)})} {e^{2 \mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : (\ell - 2)}, \eta)}} e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\eta)} .\)

By positive definiteness of \(\dot{\mathcal{G}}_u\) we have \(e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\eta)} \lesssim 1\). By construction, for all \(j \in 1 : (\ell - 2)\)

\(\displaystyle | x_{k_+} - x_{k_-} | \leq \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} | x_{k_-} - x_j | & \operatorname{if} \sigma_j = 1\\ | x_{k_+} - x_j | & \operatorname{if} \sigma_j = - 1 \end{array} \right. .\)

Thus we may bound the cross term by

\(\displaystyle 2 \mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : (\ell - 2)}, \eta) = - \beta^2 \sum_{j = 1}^{\ell - 2} \sigma_j (\mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_{k_+} - x_j) - \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_{k_-} - x_j)) \lesssim_{\ell} C\)

by applying Lemma 3.5 with the choice

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{llll} x_b := x_{k_-}, & x_g := x_{k_+} & z := x_j & \operatorname{if} \sigma_j = 1\\ x_b := x_{k_+} & x_g := x_{k_-} & z := x_j & \operatorname{if} \sigma_j = - 1. \end{array}\)

Hence we obtain \(e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \lesssim e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : (\ell - 2)})}\), and repeat the above extraction procedure inductively until only the purely charged core remains, yielding (3.14).\(\Box\)

The screening bound ensures that non-neutral configurations can be flown backward. Moving on to the neutral configurations, they are relevant and hence need to be flown forward, cf. Section 2.7. However, as the following example shows, naively changing the boundary condition does not work.

Example 3.7. (The need for localisation) Let \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\). As we have seen in the previous example, the \(\ell = 2\) neutral kernel is relevant. Trying to naively change the boundary condition and flow forward yields the equation

\(\displaystyle v^{[2]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \asymp \int_0^t e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \lambda_s^2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} s.\)

It is clear that the Steiner weight in the norm \(\unicode{x2980} \cdot \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma}\) for \(\varsigma > 0\) is incompatible with this equation. But even when \(\varsigma = 0\) we face problems: now, as \(s \leq t\), the covariance matrix behaves differently: now \(\mathcal{G}_{t, s}\) is negative, so in the neutral case the particles escape to infinity and we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x_{1 : 2}} - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) + \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (x_1 - x_2) \leq - \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0),\)

thus \(\| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \mathbb{1}_{| \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0 |} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \lambda_s^{- 2}\) and trying to flow forward

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} \unicode{x2980}_{t, 0} & \lesssim & \int_0^t \unicode{x2980}e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} \lambda_s^2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x_1 - x_2) \unicode{x2980}_{t, 0} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda_s^2 \| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x) \|_{L^1_{\eta}} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \lambda_t^2 \int_0^t \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \lambda_t^2 \end{array}\)

as the integral converges. The bound \(\unicode{x2980}v^{[2]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} \unicode{x2980}_{t, 0} \lesssim \lambda_t^2\) is worse than the expected inductive estimate \(\lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})\). That is, naively trying to flow forward in the case when the backward integral diverges, the covariance matrix will cause the forward integral to converge, breaking the induction. The problem, as we noted above, is particles escaping to infinity; evaluating \(e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})}\) at \(x_1 = x_2 = 0\) gives

\(\displaystyle e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (0)} \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} = e^{- \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0) + \beta^2 \mathcal{G}_{t, s} (0)} = 1\)

and thus formally replacing the \(L^{\infty}\) bound on \(e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})}\) with the above diagonal evaluation we obtain, with \(\mathfrak{l}_t := (1 + \log (1 \vee t))\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \int_0^t e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (0)} \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0} \unicode{x2980} \lambda_s^2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x_1 - x_2) \unicode{x2980}_{t, 0} \mathrm{d} s & \lesssim & \int_0^t \lambda_s^2 \| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x) \|_{L^1_{\eta}} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_0^t \lambda_s^2 \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \end{array}\)

with for some \(c \in \mathbb{R}_+\) when \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\), which is the expected inductive bound.

To sum up the above example, flowing forward requires forcing \(\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})\) to be evaluated on its diagonal. Implementing rigorously the idea of forcing the covariance matrix onto the diagonal in a way that also adheres to the weighted norms when \(\varsigma > 0\) requires the introduction of a localisation scheme.

The localisation scheme we employ is based on the generalisation of the Mayer expansion ansatz (3.1). Thus from now on, we work in the following generalised ansatz:

\(\displaystyle V^{[\ell], T}_t (\phi) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}} \int v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \rho (x_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \mathrm{d} \xi_{\boldsymbol{a}} \) (3.15)

where \(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}})\) are functionals of the field configuration \(\phi\) to be determined, called field content, and the index set \(\mathfrak{M}\) is called the basis of the representation, again to be determined. We shall employ the formal notation \(\langle v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} \rangle\) for terms in the above sum.

The way we determine \(\mathfrak{M}\) and \(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}})\) is inductive: we start from the \(\ell = 1\) case with assuming the naive Mayer ansatz (3.1) as before. Supposing (3.15) is already determined up to a fixed iteration order \(\ell - 1\), we compute the corresponding bilinear term on the right-hand side of (2.20) using \(V^{[0]} \equiv 0\). The so-defined source term then undergoes a post-processing based on the relevance/irrelevance of the associated kernels, involving localisation when it is needed. The basis of the extended representation at level \(\ell\) is then determined by adhering to the field contents of the possibly localised source term.

Let us carry out the above general strategy. Starting at the \(\ell = 1\) order case, we choose (3.15) to be the naive Mayer ansatz (3.1) as before; with the boundary condition \(v_{0, T}^{[1]} := \frac{\lambda}{2}\), we obtain, as in the first half of the proof of Lemma 3.3,

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v_{t, T}^{[1]} (\xi_1) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} = \lambda_t \asymp \langle t \rangle^{1 - \delta}\)

for any \(\varsigma \geq 0\). Then (3.3) gives the \(\ell = 2\) equation

\(\displaystyle \partial_t \langle v^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle + \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle = \langle \mathbf{B}^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle \) (3.16)

where we denoted

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \langle \mathbf{B}^{[2]}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}), \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle & := & - \frac{\beta^2}{2} \sigma_1 \sigma_2 \int v^{[1]}_t (\xi_1) v^{[1]}_t (\xi_2) \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2) \psi_{1 : 2} \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : 2}\\ & \asymp & \int \lambda_t^2 \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2) \psi_{1 : 2} \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : 2} \end{array}\)

For charged terms we can send this backward via the screening estimates on the covariance matrix, hence in this case the post-processing is to just leave the source term as it is.

For neutral terms, as we have seen above, this is not the way to go, hence we need to “clear” the source term \(\langle \mathbf{B}^{[2]}, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle\) from the right-hand side of (3.16). We achieve this by decomposing it into a local part that will be sent forward, and a more regular remainder that can be flown backward. Concretely, for \(\sigma_1 = - \sigma_2\), let

\(\displaystyle \psi_{1 : 2} = \frac{\psi_{1 : 2} - 1}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} | x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} + 1 =: \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} | x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} + 1\)

where \(\varkappa > 0\) to be determined. Based on this split, the source term decomposes as

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \langle \mathbf{B}^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle & = & \frac{\beta^2}{2} \int \lambda_t^2 \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2} + \frac{\beta^2}{2} \int \lambda_t^2 \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} | x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2}\\ & =: & \langle \mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_t, 1 \rangle + \langle \mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (x_{1 : 2}), \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle . \end{array}\)

Note that \(\mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t\) is a function on \((\mathbb{R}^2)^2\), while \(\mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_t\) is a number.

The above localisation thus left us with nonzero source terms corresponding to the field contents \(1, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa}\). Thus (3.16) is now replaced with the following hierarchy of equations:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{|lllll} \partial_t \langle v^{[2] (0)}_t, 1 \rangle & + & \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2] (0)}_t, 1 \rangle & = & \mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_t\\ \partial_t \langle v^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle & + & \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle & = & 0\\ \partial_t \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle & + & \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle & = & \mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \end{array}\)

The point of the localisation was that we eliminated the source term from the equation of the naive term \(\langle v^{[2]}_t, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle\), which we saw we cannot estimate in the neutral case. To ensure that it stays this way, i.e. that the action of the functional Laplacian \(\Delta_{\dot{G}_t}\) on \(\psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa}\) does not generate contributions with field content \(\psi_{1 : 2}\) (which would result in the source term of the form \(f (v^{[2], (\varkappa)}_t)\) in the equation of \(v^{[2]}_t\), reintroducing the problem we encountered in the previous example), we proceed with an abstract integration by parts argument: compute

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle & = & \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \left\langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}, \frac{\psi_{1 : 2} - 1}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} \right\rangle\\ & = & \frac{1}{2} \Delta_{\dot{G}_t} \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)} | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}, \psi_{1 : 2} - 1 \rangle\\ & \asymp & \langle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)} | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}, \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle\\ & = & \langle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)} | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}, \psi_{1 : 2} - 1 + 1 \rangle\\ & = & \langle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)}, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle + \langle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)} | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}, 1 \rangle . \end{array}\)

Hence denoting by \(\mathbf{H}_t := \partial_t + \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2})\) and

\(\displaystyle \mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t) := \langle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}, 1 \rangle := \int \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2},\)

the \(\ell = 2\) equation (3.16) in the naive representation is replaced with the following hierarchy of equations for the kernels, labelled by the field contents on the left:

\(\displaystyle \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0 : \quad \begin{array}{l|lllll} 1 & \qquad \partial_t v^{[2] (0)}_t & + & \mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t) & = & \mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_t\\ \psi_{1 : 2} & \qquad \mathbf{H}_t v^{[2]}_t & & & = & 0\\ \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} & \qquad \mathbf{H}_t v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t & & & = & \mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \end{array} \) (3.17)

Crucially, as a consequence of our post-processing, the kernel of the naive field content \(\psi_{1 : 2}\) satisfies a linear ODE with vanishing source term and zero boundary conditions, hence is identically zero.

The system (3.17) is the result of the post-processing in the neutral case. For the sake of direct comparison, we formulate the result of the above-discussed trivial post-processing in the charged case (cf. (3.16)):

\(\displaystyle \chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) \neq 0 : \quad \begin{array}{l|lll} 1 & \qquad \partial_t v^{[2] (0)}_t & = & 0\\ \psi_{1 : 2} & \qquad \mathbf{H}_t v^{[2]}_t & = & \mathbf{B}^{[2]}_t\\ \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} & \qquad \mathbf{H}_t v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t & = & 0 \end{array} \) (3.18)

We summarize the obtained representation in the following

Lemma 3.8. (Change of basis) Let \(V^{[1]}_t\) be given in terms of (3.1), and let

\(\displaystyle V^{[2]}_{t, T} (\phi) = \sum_{\sigma_{1 : 2} \in \{ - 1, 1 \}^2} \langle v^{[2] (0)}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}), 1 \rangle + \langle v^{[2]}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}), \psi_{1 : 2} \rangle + \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}), \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle\)

with kernels satisfying (3.17) when \(\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) = 0\) and (3.18) when \(\chi (\xi_{1 : 2}) \neq 0\). Then \(\{ V^{[\ell]}_{t, T} \}_{\ell \in \{ 1, 2 \}}\) satisfies the Polchinski equation (2.20) up to level \(\ell = 2\).

In this new basis, circumventing the issues highlighted in Example 3.7, we can propagate the correct inductive estimates and thus extend the result of Lemma 3.3 to the case \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi\):

Lemma 3.9. Let \(\beta^2 \in (0, 8 \pi)\), \(\varkappa \in (0, 2]\) such that \(\varkappa > \frac{\beta^2 - 4 \pi}{2 \pi} = 2 (1 - 2 \delta)\), take \(\varsigma, \eta \in \mathbb{R}_+\) arbitrary. Then imposing the boundary conditions

\(\displaystyle v^{[2]}_{T, T} := 0, \qquad v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{T, T} := 0, \qquad \operatorname{and} \quad \begin{array}{llll} v^{[2] (0)}_{T, T} & := & 0 & \operatorname{if} \quad \beta^2 < 4 \pi\\ v^{[2] (0)}_{0, T} & := & 0 & \operatorname{if} \quad \beta^2 \geq 4 \pi \end{array} \) (3.19)

on the system of equations (3.17), (3.18), we have, with \(\mathfrak{l}_t := (1 + \log (1 \vee t))\) and some \(c \in \mathbb{R}_+\), the upper bounds

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|ccc} & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (0)}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2]}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma}\\ \hline \chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) \neq 0 & 0 & \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1}) \mathfrak{l}_t^c & 0\\ \chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) = 0 & \| \rho \|_{L^1} \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1}) \mathfrak{l}_t^c & 0 & \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1}) \mathfrak{l}_t^c {\langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}}} . \end{array}\)

Moreover, for any \(\alpha \geq 0\) we have

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) | x_1 - x_2 |^{\alpha} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1}) \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa + \alpha}{2}} . \) (3.20)

Proof. Whenever a kernel is the solution of a linear ODE with vanishing source term, it vanishes identically.

In the charged case \(\chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) \neq 0\), we estimate similarly to Lemma 3.3, the only difference being the reliance on the screening bound (3.12): indeed, with \(| \chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) | = 2\)

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2]}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \leq \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[1]}_{s, T} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma}^2 \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \int_t^{\infty} \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1} .\)

In the neutral case \(\chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) = 0\), for the nonlocal remainder we use the gain from the smoothing kernel \(| \cdot |^{\varkappa}\): by Lemma A.2,

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \left\| | \cdot |^{\varkappa} \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (\cdot) e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} | \cdot |} \right\|_{L^1_{\eta}} \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 2} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \) (3.21)

which allows to send the kernel backward; with \(\| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lesssim 1\)

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{B}^{[2] (\varkappa)}_s \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \int_t^{\infty} \lambda_s^2 \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \langle s \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \) (3.22)

since the integral converges due to the condition on \(\varkappa\): indeed, \(2 (1 - \delta) - 1 - (1 - 2 \delta) - \varepsilon = - \varepsilon\). The source term of the local part is

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} & = & \left| \int_{(\mathbb{R}^2)^2} \lambda_t^2 \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2} \right|\\ & \leq & \lambda_t^2 t^{- 1} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} \| \rho \|_{L^1} \sup_{x_1} \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \rho (x_2) e^{- \frac{t | x_1 - x_2 |^2}{4}} \mathrm{d} x_2\\ & \lesssim & \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 2} \| \rho \|_{L^1} . \end{array}\)

To estimate \(\mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t)\), note that in the neutral case

\(\displaystyle \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \asymp_{\beta} - (\dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (0) - \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2))\)

so that with \(1 \leq \omega_t^{\varsigma}\) it follows from Lemma A.3 that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t) \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} & = & \left| \int_{(\mathbb{R}^2)^2} \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2} \right|\\ & \lesssim & \left\| \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} \right\|_{L^{\infty}} \int_{(\mathbb{R}^2)^2} | v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) | \rho (x_{1 : 2}) \omega_t^{\varsigma} (x_{1 : 2}) \langle \operatorname{St} (x_{1 : 2}) \rangle^{\eta} \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2}\\ & \leq & \left\| \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} \right\|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \| \rho \|_{L^1}\\ & \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1 + \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \| \rho \|_{L^1} \end{array}\)

Hence, since by locality \(| v^{[2] (0)}_t | = \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (0)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} = \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (0)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma}\) for all \(s, t \in \mathbb{R}_+\), when \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi \Leftrightarrow 1 - \delta < 1 / 2\) the irrelevant boundary condition yields

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (0)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \int_t^{\infty} \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_s) \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} + \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_s \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \| \rho \|_{L^1} \int_t^{\infty} \lambda_s^2 \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \| \rho \|_{L^1} \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1}\)

since the integral converges, while if \(\beta^2 \geq 4 \pi \Leftrightarrow 1 - \delta \geq 1 / 2\) the relevant boundary condition yields

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (0)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \int_0^t \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{A} (v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_s) \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} + \unicode{x2980}\mathbf{B}^{[2] (0)}_s \unicode{x2980}_{s, \varsigma} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \| \rho \|_{L^1} \int_0^t \lambda_s^2 \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \mathrm{d} s \lesssim \| \rho \|_{L^1} \lambda_t^2 \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_t\)

since the integral diverges. Finally, (3.20) follows from (3.21) and (3.22) by replacing \(\varkappa\) with \(\varkappa + \alpha\).\(\Box\)

Remark 3.10. Unifying the notation, with the above lemma, we have proven in the \(\ell = 2\) case the inductive bound

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma, \eta} \lesssim_{\rho} \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c {\langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa}{2}}} , \qquad \varkappa \in (0, 2] \cup \{ 0 \}\)

which holds even for \(v^{[2]}\) if one insists on it corresponding to \(\varkappa = 0\) by an abuse of notation (as we have seen, even though \(v^{[2]}\) and its localisation \(v^{[2] (0)}\) satisfy the same estimates, it is crucial that we treat them as different kernels in order to prove said estimates).

Also note that the estimate depends on \(\rho\) only in the case of the vacuum energy term \(v^{[2] (0)}\).

Remark 3.11. For future reference, we record that as \(\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) = - \beta^2 (\dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (0) + \sigma_1 \sigma_2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_2))\),

\(\displaystyle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) = \frac{\beta^2}{2} \int_t^T e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : 2})} \lambda_s^2 \rho (x_{1 : 2}) | x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} \sigma_1 \sigma_2 \dot{\mathcal{G}}_s (x_1 - x_2) \mathrm{d} s\)

is symmetric under charge conjugation i.e. under \(\sigma_{1 : 2} \leftrightarrow \overline{\sigma_{1 : 2}} := - \sigma_{1 : 2}\).

The previous two Lemmas tell us that in the the charged case \(\chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) \neq 0\) the field content is \(\psi_{1 : 2}\), hence it propagates the field content \(\psi_{1 : \ell}\) further down the induction, i.e. for any \(V^{[| I |]} = \langle v^{[| I |]}, \psi_{| I |} \rangle\), the bilinear term

\(\displaystyle \langle \mathrm{D} V^{[2]}_t \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) \neq 0}, \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V^{[| I |]}_t \rangle\)

will have associated field content \(\psi_{| I | + 2}\). On the other hand, the neutral term has the dipole field content \(\{ 1, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \}\). Compute

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{D} (\langle v^{[2] (0)}_t, 1 \rangle + \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} \rangle) = v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} \psi_{1 : 2} \sum_{j = 1}^2 \sigma_j \delta_{x_j} i \beta \) (3.23)

so that for example

\(\displaystyle \langle \mathrm{D} V^{[2]}_t \mathbb{1}_{\chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) = 0}, \dot{G}_t \mathrm{D} V^{[1]}_t \rangle \asymp \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_3) v^{[1]}, | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} \psi_{1 : 2} \psi_3 \rangle .\)

Now a priori we have two options:

  1. we try to absorb the divergence \(| x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}\) into \(\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_3)\), leaving us with the field content \(\psi_{1 : 3}\).

  2. create a new dipole, i.e. pick a \(\tilde{\varkappa}\) so that

    \(\displaystyle | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} \psi_{1 : 2} \psi_3 = | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} (\psi_{1 : 2} - 1 + 1) \psi_3 = | x_1 - x_2 |^{\tilde{\varkappa} - \varkappa} \psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3 + \frac{\psi_3}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}},\)

    which yields the decomposition

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \langle \mathrm{D} V^{[2]}_t \mathbb{1}_{| \chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) | = 0}, \dot{G} \mathrm{D} V^{[1]}_t \rangle & \asymp & \langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_3) v^{[1]}, | x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa} \psi_{1 : 2} \psi_3 \rangle\\ & = & \left\langle \int v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (x_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_3)}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[1]} (x_3) \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2}, \psi_3 \right\rangle\\ & + & \left\langle v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_3)}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa - \tilde{\varkappa}}} v^{[1]}, \psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3 \right\rangle\\ & =: & \langle \mathbf{B}_{\psi_3} (x_3), \psi_3 \rangle + \langle \mathbf{B}_{\psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3} (x_{1 : 3}), \psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3 \rangle . \end{array} \) (3.24)

    This induces a change of basis in the \(\ell = 3\), \(| \chi (\sigma_{1 : 3}) | = 1\) case, which would then create further modifications along the induction in \(\ell\), making the representation (3.15) differ significantly from the classical Mayer ansatz (3.1).

It turns out that in the case \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\), since we may choose \(\varkappa := 1\) (cf. Lemma 3.9), option 1. is viable: it is possible to negate the divergence \(| x_1 - x_2 |^{- 1}\) of the dipole, hence returning to the Mayer ansatz from \(\ell \geq 3\) onwards. This is shown in the following

Lemma 3.12. Let \(\varkappa \in (0, 1]\), \(\varsigma, \eta \geq 0\), \(c > \varsigma\). Suppose \(\xi_{1 : 2}\) is neutral, i.e. \(\sigma_1 = - \sigma_2\). Then for any kernel \(k_t (\xi_I)\), \(I \subseteq 3 : \ell\)

\(\displaystyle \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_I)}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} k_t (\xi_I) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- 2 + \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \unicode{x2980}k_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} . \) (3.25)

Moreover, when \(\xi_{3 : 4}\) is also neutral, i.e. \(\sigma_3 = - \sigma_4\),

\(\displaystyle \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{3 : 4})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} | x_3 - x_4 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{3 : 4}) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{t, \varsigma} \lesssim \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- 2 + \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \) (3.26)

Proof. To prove (3.25), fix \(j \in I\) and denote by \(y := x_j\). Then using Lemma A.6 with \(| \mu | = 0\) we have for any \(c \geq 0\)

\(\displaystyle \frac{| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - y) - \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_2 - y) |}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} \lesssim | x_1 - x_2 |^{1 - \varkappa} e^{- c \sqrt{t} | x_1 - y |} \omega_t^c (x_{1 : 2}) e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} t^{- \frac{1}{2}}\)

thus writing \(\delta_{i j} := | x_i - x_j |\) it follows from (3.7) and \(\tilde{d} (x_{1 : 2}, x_I) \leq | x_1 - y |\) that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_I)}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} k_t (\xi_I) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{t, \varsigma}\\ \lesssim & \sup_{x_1} \int | v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \omega^{\varsigma}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) | \sum_{j \in I} \frac{| \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_1 - x_j) - \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_2 - x_j) |}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} e^{\varsigma \sqrt{t} \tilde{d} (x_{1 : 2}, x_I)} | k_t (\xi_I) \omega^{\varsigma}_t (\xi_I) | \mathrm{d} x_2 \mathrm{d} x_I\\ \lesssim & \sum_{j \in I} \unicode{x2980}k_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \sup_{x_1} \int | v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \omega_t^{\varsigma + c} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \delta_{12}^{1 - \varkappa} | e^{(\varsigma - c) \sqrt{t} \delta_{1 j}} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} t^{- \frac{1}{2}} \mathrm{d} x_2 \mathrm{d} x_j\\ \lesssim & \sum_{j \in I} \unicode{x2980}k_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | \cdot |^{1 - \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \sup_{x_1} \int e^{(\varsigma - c) \sqrt{t} \delta_{1 j}} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} t^{- \frac{1}{2}} \mathrm{d} x_j\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}k_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{1 - \varkappa}{2}} \langle t \rangle^{- 1 - \frac{1}{2}} \end{array}\)

where in the last estimate we used (3.20) and that \(c > \varsigma\). Thus we proved (3.25) for \(\eta = 0\), and the same argument extends for any \(\eta \geq 0\) after inserting \(\langle \operatorname{St} (\cdot) \rangle^{\eta}\).

For (3.26) we proceed similarly: assuming, without loss of generality that \(\sigma_1 = \sigma_3 = 1\) and \(\sigma_2 = \sigma_4 = - 1\), we have, writing \(\vartheta (x_{34}) := x_3 + \vartheta (x_4 - x_3)\), we have by Lemma A.6 with \(| \mu | = 1\) that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{3 : 4})}{\delta_{12}^{\varkappa} \delta_{34}^{\varkappa}}\\ \asymp_{\beta} & \frac{\dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_3 - x_1) - \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_4 - x_1)}{\delta_{12}^{\varkappa} \delta_{34}^{\varkappa}} - \frac{\dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_3 - x_2) - \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (x_4 - x_2)}{\delta_{12}^{\varkappa} \delta_{34}^{\varkappa}}\\ = & \delta_{12}^{- \varkappa} \delta_{34}^{- \varkappa} (x_3 - x_4) \cdot \int_0^1 \nabla \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (\vartheta (x_{34}) - x_1) - \nabla \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t (\vartheta (x_{34}) - x_2) \mathrm{d} \vartheta\\ \lesssim & e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} \delta_{12}^{1 - \varkappa} \delta_{34}^{1 - \varkappa} \int_0^1 e^{- c \sqrt{t} | x_1 - \vartheta (x_{34}) |} e^{c \sqrt{t} \delta_{12}} \mathrm{d} \vartheta\\ \lesssim & e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} \delta_{12}^{1 - \varkappa} \delta_{34}^{1 - \varkappa} e^{- c \sqrt{t} \delta_{13}} \omega^c_t (x_{1 : 2}) \omega^c_t (x_{3 : 4}) \end{array}\)

where the last bound follows from

\(\displaystyle | x_1 - x_3 | - \vartheta | x_4 - x_3 | \leq | x_1 - x_3 - \vartheta (x_4 - x_3) | = | x_1 - \vartheta (x_{34}) | .\)

Thus we have, with \(K_t (i, j) := | v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_i, \xi_j) | \omega_t^{\varsigma + c} (x_i, x_j) \delta_{i j}^{1 - \varkappa}\)

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_t (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{3 : 4})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} | x_3 - x_4 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t (\xi_{3 : 4}) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{t, \varsigma}\\ \lesssim & \sup_{x_1} \int K_t (1, 2) e^{(\varsigma - c) \sqrt{t} \delta_{13}} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} K_t (3, 4) \mathrm{d} x_{2 : 4}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | \cdot |^{1 - \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \sup_{x_1} \int K_t (1, 2) e^{(\varsigma - c) \sqrt{t} \delta_{13}} e^{- \frac{m^2}{t}} \mathrm{d} x_{2 : 3}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | \cdot |^{1 - \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t | \cdot |^{1 - \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- 1}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{1 - \varkappa}{2}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\varkappa)}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \varsigma + c} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{1 - \varkappa}{2}} \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \end{array}\)

by (3.20) and \(c > \varsigma\). Again, the argument generalizes to any \(\eta \geq 0\) after inserting \(\langle \operatorname{St} (\cdot) \rangle^{\eta}\).\(\Box\)

Remark 3.13. (Above \(6 \pi\)) When \(\beta^2 > 6 \pi\), i.e. we need to choose \(\varkappa \in (1, 2]\), Lemma 3.12 cannot be used. We then have to choose option 2 in the discussion preceding the lemma, creating a “tripole” in (3.24). The point is that the remainder bilinear term \(\mathbf{B}_{\psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3} (x_{1 : 3})\) has a lowered divergence \(| x_1 - x_2 |^{- (\varkappa - \tilde{\varkappa})}\); it turns out that we may pick \(\tilde{\varkappa} := 1\) so that \(\varkappa - \tilde{\varkappa} \in (0, 1]\) and Lemma 3.12 can be used to estimate \(\mathbf{B}_{\psi_{1 : 2}^{\tilde{\varkappa}} \psi_3}\); on the other hand, \(\mathbf{B}_{\psi_3} (x_3)\) has integration over \(\mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2}\) which provides the additional smoothing effect to negate the divergence \(| x_1 - x_2 |^{- \varkappa}\). As we have mentioned, the price to pay is the proliferating change of bases further along the flow, which results in a representation for \(V^{[\ell]}\) significantly different from the naive Mayer ansatz. We refer to [36] for further discussion on this issue.

Note that we introduced in (3.5) the function \(\Theta\) in the weight, but so far we only took norms with \(\Theta := \varsigma \in \mathbb{R}_+\). Since the above lemma changes the weight in the norm, this is no longer feasible. Hence from now on, we take (3.5) with

\(\displaystyle \Theta := \Theta (\ell) := 4^{- \ell} .\)

Lemma 3.12 allows us to finally determine the form of the generalised representation (3.15) for all \(\ell \geq 3\), and subsequently to propagate the induction through the corresponding kernels, i.e. to extend Lemma 3.3 from \(\beta^2 < 4 \pi\) to \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\). Define the basis of representation

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{M} := \{ (\ell, \sigma_{1 : \ell}, \varkappa) : \ell \in \mathbb{N}_0, \sigma_{1 : \ell} \in \{ - 1, 1 \}^{\ell}, \varkappa \in \{ 0, 1 \} \} .\)

Given an index \(\boldsymbol{a} := (\ell, \sigma_{1 : \ell}, \varkappa) \in \mathfrak{M}\), let us introduce the notation

\(\displaystyle \ell (\boldsymbol{a}) := \ell, \qquad \sigma (\boldsymbol{a}) := \sigma_{1 : \ell}, \qquad \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) := \varkappa .\)

We decompose the basis as \(\mathfrak{M} = \mathfrak{M}_p \uplus (\mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p)\), where \(\mathfrak{M}_p\) is the subset of indices that needed nontrivial post-processing, namely

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{M}_p := \{ (2, \sigma_{1 : 2}, \varkappa) : \chi (\sigma_{1 : 2}) = 0 \}\)

The associated field content of the representation is then

\(\displaystyle \Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} := \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} \psi_{1 : \ell} & \operatorname{if} & \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p, \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 0\\ 0 & \operatorname{if} & \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p, \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 1\\ \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} & \operatorname{if} & \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}_p \end{array} \right. \qquad \operatorname{where} \quad \psi_{1 : 2}^{\varkappa} := \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} \frac{\psi_{1 : 2} - 1}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} & \operatorname{if} & \varkappa > 0\\ 1 & \operatorname{if} & \varkappa = 0. \end{array} \right. \) (3.27)

We note that this is a redefinition of our previous notation; we have already started moving in this direction in Remark 3.10.

The boundary conditions are already determined for almost all kernels of the first \(2\) iteration orders, cf. (3.19) and \(v_{0, T}^{[1] (\boldsymbol{a})} := \frac{\lambda}{2}\) when \(\varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 0\); what remains is setting

\(\displaystyle v_{0, T}^{[1] (\boldsymbol{a})} := 0 \quad \operatorname{if} \quad \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 1.\)

For all kernels with \(\ell \geq 3\) we impose the irrelevant boundary condition, i.e. we set

\(\displaystyle v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{T, T} := 0 \quad \operatorname{if} \quad \ell \geq 3, \quad \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p .\) (3.28)

With this, the boundary conditions have been fixed for all kernels of the index set \(\mathfrak{M}\).

Having determined the generalised ansatz (3.15), we can now translate the Polchinski equation (2.20) from \(V^{[\ell], T}_t\) to a hierarchy of equations for the kernels \(v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\) of the representation and prove the inductive bounds on them.

Proposition 3.14. (estimates on the potential kernels) Let \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\). Then there exist kernels \(\{ v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \}_{\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}}\) obeying boundary conditions \(v_{0, T}^{[1] (\boldsymbol{a})} := \frac{\lambda}{2}\), (3.19) and (3.28), such that \(\{ V^{[\ell], T}_t \}_{\ell}\), given by (3.15), satisfy (2.20). Moreover, for each \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}\) and any \(\eta \in \mathbb{R}_+\), we have the estimates

\(\displaystyle \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta (\ell), \eta} \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{\varkappa (\boldsymbol{a})}{2}} \) (3.29)

for some \(c = c (\boldsymbol{a}, \beta^2) \in \mathbb{R}_+\). All bounds are independent of \(\rho\) except that of \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}_p\) with \(\varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 0\).

Proof. First, we set \(v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{\cdot, T} \equiv 0\) whenever \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\) and \(\varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 1\). These trivially satisfy (3.29) and also consistent with the Polchinski equation because of the \(0\) field content. Thus from now on we only consider the remaining kernels.

The starting level \(\ell = 1\) is done just as in the proof of Lemma 3.3. The case \(\ell = 2\) has been treated in Lemma 3.9 (cf. also Remark 3.10 for notation). Thus from now on, suppose \(\ell \geq 3\); we will check (3.29) inductively with the understanding that \(c \in \mathbb{R}_+\) in \(\mathfrak{l}_t^c\) is a constant we do not track and may change from line to line.

Based on Lemma 3.8, the bilinear part of the Polchinski equation (2.20) may be decomposed as

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \sum_{\ell' + \ell'' = \ell} \langle \mathrm{D} V^{[\ell']}_s, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} V_s^{[\ell'']} \rangle\\ = & \sum_{\ell' + \ell'' = \ell} \left\langle \sum_{\boldsymbol{b} \in \mathfrak{M}} C_{\boldsymbol{b}} \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[\ell'] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{b}} \rangle, \dot{G}_s \sum_{\boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M}} C_{\boldsymbol{c}} \langle \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[\ell''] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{c}} \rangle \rangle \right\rangle\\ = & \left( \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} \ell' + \ell'' = \ell\\ \boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p \end{array}$}}} + \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} \ell' + \ell'' = \ell\\ \boldsymbol{b} \in \mathfrak{M}_p, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p \end{array}$}}} + \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} \ell' + \ell'' = \ell\\ \boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M}_p \end{array}$}}} \right) C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}} \langle \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[\ell'] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{b}} \rangle, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[\ell''] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{c}} \rangle \rangle \end{array}\)

with \(C_{\boldsymbol{b}}, C_{\boldsymbol{c}}, C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}}\) nonnegative combinatorial constants. We treat the three parts in separate cases.

  1. Case \(\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\): by (3.27) we compute, just as in the proof of Lemma 3.1, that

    \(\displaystyle \langle \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{b}} \rangle, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{c}} \rangle \rangle = C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}} \langle v^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s (\xi_{I_2}), \psi_{1 : \ell} \rangle\)

    for some constant \(C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}}\), so that

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})} (\xi_{I_1}) \dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{I_1}, \xi_{I_2}) v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} (\xi_{I_2}) \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (\ell)}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- \ell}} \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- \ell}}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (| I_1 |)} \langle s \rangle^{- 2} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (| I_2 |)}\\ \lesssim & \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \end{array}\)

  2. Case \(\boldsymbol{b} \in \mathfrak{M}_p, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\): computing as in (3.23),

    \(\displaystyle \langle \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{b}} \rangle, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{c}} \rangle \rangle = C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}} \left\langle v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{I_2})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s (\xi_{I_2}), \psi_{1 : \ell} \right\rangle\)

    where \(\varkappa := \varkappa (\boldsymbol{b}) = 1\). Hence estimating by (3.25) with \(c := 2 \cdot 4^{- \ell}\) and using the bounds of Lemma 3.9 we get, since \(\ell \geq 3\)

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{I_2})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} (\xi_{I_2}) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{s, \Theta (\ell)}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 3 \cdot 4^{- \ell}} \langle s \rangle^{- 2 + \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- \ell}}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- (\ell - 1)}} \langle s \rangle^{- 2 + \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- \ell}}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (2)} \langle s \rangle^{- 2 + \frac{\varkappa}{2}} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (| I_2 |)}\\ \lesssim & \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_s^c . \end{array}\)

  3. Case \(\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c} \in \mathfrak{M}_p\): it follows from the definition of \(\mathfrak{M}_p\) that this only happens in when \(\ell = 4\). Similarly to (3.23) we obtain

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \langle \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_1 |] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{b}} \rangle, \dot{G}_s \mathrm{D} \langle v^{[| I_2 |] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s, \Psi_{\boldsymbol{c}} \rangle \rangle\\ = & C_{\boldsymbol{b}, \boldsymbol{c}} \left\langle v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{3 : 4})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} | x_3 - x_4 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s (\xi_{3 : 4}), \psi_{1 : 4} \right\rangle \end{array}\)

    where \(\varkappa := \varkappa (\boldsymbol{b}) = \varkappa (\boldsymbol{c}) = 1\). Thus estimating by (3.26) with \(c := 2 \cdot 4^{- \ell}\) and Lemma 3.9

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \left\vert\!\left\vert\!\left\vert v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\dot{\mathbb{G}}_s (\xi_{1 : 2}, \xi_{3 : 4})}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa} | x_3 - x_4 |^{\varkappa}} v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s (\xi_{3 : 4}) \right\vert\!\right\vert\!\right\vert_{s, \Theta (\ell)}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- (\ell - 1)}} \langle s \rangle^{- 2 + \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{c})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, 4^{- (\ell - 1)}}\\ \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v_s^{[2] (\boldsymbol{b})} \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (2)} \langle s \rangle^{- 2 + \varkappa} \unicode{x2980}v^{[2] (\boldsymbol{c})}_s \unicode{x2980}_{s, \Theta (2)}\\ \lesssim & \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_s^c . \end{array}\)

Hence combining the above cases and using the screening bound (3.12) we get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_t \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta (\ell)} & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} \| e^{\mathbb{G}_{t, s} (\xi_{1 : \ell})} \|_{L^{\infty}} \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \int_t^{\infty} (\lambda_t \lambda_s^{- 1})^{| \chi (\xi_{1 : \ell}) |} \lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \langle s \rangle^{- 1} \mathfrak{l}_s^c \mathrm{d} s\\ & \lesssim & \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c . \end{array}\)

where the integral converges because the assumption \(\ell \geq 3\) implies either \(\ell = 3\), in which case \(| \chi (\sigma_{1 : \ell}) | \geq 1\), or \(\ell \geq 4\), in which case \(\lambda_s (\lambda_s \langle s \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \asymp \langle s \rangle^{1 - 4 \delta} \lesssim \langle s \rangle^{- \varepsilon}\) for some \(\varepsilon > 0\) as \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi \Leftrightarrow \delta > 1 / 4\).\(\Box\)

A straightforward consequence of the above uniform bounds is the removal of the UV cutoff \(T\). To this end, we extend the kernels trivially from \([0, T]\) to \(\mathbb{R}_+\); namely, put

\(\displaystyle \tilde{v}^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} := v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t \wedge T, T}, \) (3.30)

for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\); we abuse notation by simply writing \(v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\) instead of \(\tilde{v}^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\).

Corollary 3.15. For any \(\eta \geq 0\), the \(\unicode{x2980} \cdot \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta}\)-limits

\(\displaystyle v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, \infty} := \lim_{T \rightarrow \infty} v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T}\)

exist, are independent of \(\eta\), and satisfy the bounds (3.29). Moreover, \(V^{[\ell], \infty, \rho} (\phi)\), given by

\(\displaystyle V^{[\ell], \infty, \rho}_t := \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}\\ \ell (\boldsymbol{a}) = \ell \end{array}$}}} V^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), \infty, \rho}_t \qquad \operatorname{where} \qquad V^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), \infty, \rho}_t (\phi) := \int v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, \infty} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \Psi_{\boldsymbol{a}} (\xi_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \rho (x_{\boldsymbol{a}}) \mathrm{d} \xi_{\boldsymbol{a}} \) (3.31)

satisfies (2.20).

Proof. See Appendix B.3\(\Box\)

To simplify notation, we frequently suppress reference to \(\rho\), and also write \(v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_t := v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, \infty}\) and \(V^{[\ell]}_t := V^{[\ell], \infty}_t\).

4The effective force observable

With the inductive bounds on the kernels of the representation proven, we may translate them to estimates on the approximate effective force. We tacitly assume that every object with superscript \(T\) is extended from \([0, T]\) to \(\mathbb{R}_+\) trivially, i.e. just as in (3.30) above.

Proposition 4.1. (estimates on the approximate force) Let \(\beta^2 < 6 \pi\). Denote by \(E := L^{\infty}\) or \(L^2 (\langle x \rangle^{- n})\) for \(n > 2\). Then with \(F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t {:= \mathrm{D} V^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t} \), for all \(X \in \mathcal{} \mathcal{S}'\), we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_T {\sup_{\rho}}_{_{}} \sup_{\phi \in \mathcal{H}} \| Q_t F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi) \|_E \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- 1} . \) (4.1)
\(\displaystyle \sup_T \sup_{\rho_{}} \sup_{\phi \in \mathcal{H}} \| \langle \mathrm{D} F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi), \dot{G}_t X \rangle \|_E \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- 1} {\| Q_t X \|_E}_{} . \) (4.2)

Moreover, denoting \(E := L^{\infty}\) or \(L^2 (\langle x \rangle^{- n})\) for any \(n \in \mathbb{Z}\), we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_T {\sup_{\rho}}_{_{}} \| Q_t (F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi) - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi')) \|_E \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \| \phi - \phi' \|_E \) (4.3)
\(\displaystyle \sup_T \sup_{\rho_{}} \| \langle \mathrm{D} (F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi) - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t (\phi')), \dot{G}_t X \rangle \|_E \lesssim \lambda_t (\lambda_t \langle t \rangle^{- 1})^{\ell - 1} \mathfrak{l}_t^c \langle t \rangle^{- 1} {\| Q_t X \|_{L^{\infty}}}_{} \| \phi - \phi' \|_E \) (4.4)

Finally, if \(\| Q_t X \|_{L^{\infty}} < \infty\), then as \(T \rightarrow \infty\),

\(\displaystyle {\sup_{\rho}}_{_{}} \sup_{\phi \in \mathcal{H}} \| Q_t (F^{[\ell], \infty, \rho}_t - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t) (\phi) \|_{L^{\infty}} + \| \langle \mathrm{D} (F^{[\ell], \infty, \rho}_t - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t) (\phi), \dot{G}_t X \rangle \|_{L^{\infty}} \rightarrow 0.\)

and for \(n > 2\), denoting by \(L^{2, - n} := L^2 (\langle x \rangle^{- n})\), as \(\rho \rightarrow \mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{R}^2}\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_T \sup_{\phi \in \mathcal{H}} \left\| Q_t \left( F^{[\ell], T, \mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{R}^2}}_t - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t \right) (\phi) \right\|_{L^{2, - n}} + \left\| \left\langle \mathrm{D} \left( F^{[\ell], T, \mathbb{1}_{\mathbb{R}^2}}_t - F^{[\ell], T, \rho}_t \right) (\phi), \dot{G}_t X \right\rangle \right\|_{L^{2, - n}} \rightarrow 0.\)

Proof. The bounds follow from the estimates of the previous section in a straightforward way.

  1. The force bound (4.1):

    The vacuum energy contribution \(v^{[2] (0)}_{t, T}\) is annihilated by the derivative, thus denoting by

    \(\displaystyle M^{(\boldsymbol{b})} (x_I) := \mathbb{1}_{\boldsymbol{b} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p} + \mathbb{1}_{\boldsymbol{b} \in \mathfrak{M}_p} \mathbb{1}_{\varkappa (\boldsymbol{b}) > 0} | x_{I (1)} - x_{I (2)} |^{- \varkappa (\boldsymbol{b})}\)

    we compute

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} Q_t F^{[\ell], T}_t (\phi) (y) & = & Q_t \mathrm{D} V^{[\ell], T}_t (\phi) (y)\\ & = & i \beta \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}\\ \ell (\boldsymbol{a}) = \ell \end{array}$}}} \int \rho (x_{1 : \ell}) v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} M^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \sum_{j = 1}^{\ell} \sigma_j k^Q_t (x_j - y) \psi_{1 : \ell} \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : \ell} . \end{array}\)

    Hence for \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\), we simply have, uniformly in \(\| \rho \|_{L^{\infty}} \leq 1\), that

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \| Q_t F^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), T}_t (\phi) \|_{L^{\infty}} & \lesssim & \sup_y \int | v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) | \sum_{j = 1}^{\ell} k^Q_t (x_j - y) \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : \ell}\\ & \leq & \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} \sup_y \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} k^Q_t (x - y) \mathrm{d} x\\ & \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} \langle t \rangle^{- 1}, \end{array}\)

    On the other hand, for \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}_p\) with \(\varkappa := \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 1\), by neutrality \(\sigma_1 = - \sigma_2\), using Lemma A.6, and (3.20) yields

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} | Q_t F^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), T}_t (\phi) (y) | & \lesssim & \int v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{\sigma_1 k^Q_t (x_1 - y) + \sigma_2 k^Q_t (x_2 - y)}{| x_1 - x_2 |^{\varkappa}} \psi_{1 : 2} \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : 2}\\ & \lesssim & \int v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} (\xi_{1 : 2}) \omega_t^c (x_{1 : 2}) e^{- c \sqrt{t} | x_1 - y |} e^{- \frac{m^2}{2 t}} t^{\frac{1}{2}} \mathrm{d} x_{1 : 2}\\ & \lesssim & \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{1}{2}} \end{array}\)

    and the \(L^{\infty}\) statement follows from Proposition 3.14 and Corollary 3.15. The \(L^2 (\langle x \rangle^{- n})\) statement is done verbatim.

  2. The contraction bound (4.2):

    Since \(\dot{G}_t X = \dot{\mathcal{G}}_t \ast X \in \mathcal{C}^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^d)\), point evaluations make sense and we may compute

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} & \langle \mathrm{D} F^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), T}_t (\phi), \dot{G}_t X \rangle (y)\\ = & \mathrm{D} F^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a}), T}_t (\phi) [\dot{G}_t X] (y)\\ = & - \beta^2 \int \rho (x_{1 : \ell}) v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} M^{(\boldsymbol{a})} (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \sum_{j, k = 1}^{\ell} \sigma_j \sigma_k (\dot{G}_t X) (x_j) \delta (x_k - y) \psi_{1 : \ell} \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : \ell} . \end{array}\)

    When \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\), we simply bound it by

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} \lesssim & \| \dot{G}_t X \|_{L^{\infty}} \int | v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} | (\xi_{1 : \ell}) \sum_{k = 1}^{\ell} \delta (x_k - y) \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : \ell}\\ \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- 1} \| Q_t X \|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} . \end{array}\)

    and we conclude from Proposition 3.14 and Corollary 3.15. When \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M}_p\) with \(\varkappa := \varkappa (\boldsymbol{a}) = 1\), by neutrality \(\sigma_1 = - \sigma_2\) it is estimated via

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} \lesssim & \int | v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} | (\xi_{1 : 2}) \frac{| (\dot{G}_t X) (x_1) - (\dot{G}_t X) (x_2) |}{| x_1 - x_2 |} \sum_{k = 1}^2 \delta (x_k - y) \mathrm{d} \xi_{1 : 2}\\ \lesssim & \| \nabla \dot{G}_t X \|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta}\\ \lesssim & \langle t \rangle^{- \frac{1}{2}} \| Q_t X \|_{L^{\infty}} \unicode{x2980}v^{[\ell] (\boldsymbol{a})}_{t, T} \unicode{x2980}_{t, \Theta, \eta} \end{array}\)

    by (A.2).

  3. The Lipschitz bounds (4.3) and (4.4):

    Denoting by \(\psi'_j := e^{i \beta \sigma_j \phi' (x_j)}\), we have

    \(\displaystyle | \psi_{1 : \ell} - \psi'_{1 : \ell} | = \sum_{j \in 1 : \ell} | e^{i \beta \sigma_j \phi (x_j)} - e^{i \beta \sigma_j \phi' (x_j)} | \leq \beta \sum_{j \in 1 : \ell} | \phi (x_j) - \phi' (x_j) |\)

    from which the \(L^{\infty}\) bounds follow immediately by repeating the argument of (4.1) and (4.2). Thus from now on, we focus only on the weighted \(L^2\) bounds. First let \(\boldsymbol{a} \in \mathfrak{M} \setminus \mathfrak{M}_p\). Fix a charge configuration \(\sigma_{1 : \ell}\) and pick any \(j, k \in 1 : \ell\); we have, with the change of variables \(r = x_j - y\), \(z_j = 0\), \(z_m = x_m - x_j\) (\(m \neq j\))
